NOTER
AND RECORDS
information and comment regarding the Cabinet changes
in Japan.
I would call especial attention to the statement
that there has been a contest between the Emperor's
FW
personal advisers and the Army, the former having striven
894.00/89
"to bring about the selection as Prime Minister of a
general army officer in active service with a view to
placing responsibility for the adjustment of pressing
economic and military problems squarely on the Army,
whereas it has been the tactics of the Army to avoid
2
such responsibility"; and to the statement, "In the
opinion of qualified observers, the Army must eventually
assume entire responsibility for the conduct of the
Government ...."
Comment:
In the light of the latter of these statements, a
statement of opinion -- in which I concur -- I feel that
we should regard the present Cabinet as one not likely
to achieve great success and to live long. It may, as
suggested

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.97)
121294
- 2 -
suggested further along in the telegram, "make a bold
attempt to place the Army under the definite control
of its more moderate elements"; but if it does that,
the chances are that it will not succeed. I share Mr.
Grew's opinion that, "From point of view of our rela-
tions with Japan the appointment of Yonai is as satis-
factory as could be hoped for"; also, I concur in his
estimate that we need "not anticipate any immediate
drastic revision of Japan's objectives and actions in
China"; but I do not share the optimism of his, "I look
for steady and progressive moderation of scope and
methods" (unless "scope and methods" be construed as
referring only to small matters in the field of opera-
tions as contrasted with large matters in the field of
objectives and major strategy). [One thing, we may
reasonably expect: that Admiral Yonai will oppose entry
by Japan upon courses of close cooperation with the
axis powers or with the Soviet Union.]

Time after time in the Russo-Japanese Wer, situations developed in
which, had the Russians launched a counterattack, the Japanese forces would
have been annihilated. (126)
The one thing certain in this world is change. Methods of fighting
change as well as all else. Countries which fail to see this fact and take
advantage of it ay in blood and treesure.
With the coming of increused transportation rscilities and ample ammumi-
tion stocks through industrialized methods, an the use of gas in denying arees
to the attacker, the value of ground defenses is more importent then ever before.
Infantry troops only cen hold ground guined, but economy of life teaches that
machines can best prepare the way for the infantry.
Mechanized and air forces heve ceused the nain etriking ower of an ermy
to shift from men to animale to mechines.
To wege & successful war, a nation must employ all ite resources. The
United States has a genius for mamufacture. She can turn out more mechines than
the rest of the world combined. Young America is mechanically inclined. For the
United States t fail to take advantage of this mochanical superiority is failure
to use her resources intelligently. (127)
By the design of a mount suitable t. make & light-fust-tenk out of com-
merical motor car chassis, the United Stetes could heve severel million light
fast tanks in & year. By subsidizinf still furthor our air mail eystem, so es
to carry all irst cless mail by sir, the United Stetes would heve ample well-trained
air pilots.
The will of a country can no longer be cuntured on the battlefield.
Decision is easiest and uickest in the heart of e nation. (l28) The tremen-
dous striking power of en air force directed st the  per cities of congested
Japan is an offensive weapon of greet value.
Japanese soldier psychology inspires confiuence, but it is based on
supremacy of man power over men nower. Javan has never faced o determined army,
equipped to slaughter in the modern manner. Confronted by a thoroughly modern
army, Japan's man army will be found t be as ineffective as were her Samursi
warriors against Perry's black ships.
The Japanese psychology knows only victory. Only in victory do they
press on — mistakes and reverses drive them to muicide.
Anglo-Saxons rise t their createst deods un er pressure and adversity.
It is the excitement of the crisis which carries them through. Not so with the
Japanose. In adversity their worst traits crop out. (129)

In spite of the brilliant record of our American Army in the World
Wer, at its close the records show thet the Aray of th Jnited States carried
four hundred an seventy thousand men in cosertion! (1)
This dismaying totel ws tecumtlated in spite of service flegs hang-

itary orders such as: "The outpost will hold until relieved by the Division,"
requires the highest type of leadershin. A sollier, knowing he 1s likely never
again to see daylisht if he does hold his outpost egainst enemy ire, wondere

Por centuries Chine held her soldiers lowest in the social scsle. Sho

When the Unitod States sent Commodore Perry to Japan in 1853, we took
no half-hearted measures such as sacrificing missionaries to the fate of the
Spanish proselyters of 1624 and 1638.
In the words of the Herald-Tribune of the time: "The Jananese expedi-
tion, sccording to a Weshington correspondent, is to be merely a hydrographical
survey of the Japanese coast. The 32-pounders are to be used merely es meesuring
instruments in the triangulationa; the canmon-balls are for procuring bese lines.
If any Japanese is foolish enough to ut his head in the way of these meteorol-
ogicel instruments, of course nobody wil be to blame but himself if he should
get hurt."
And tho London Punch said: "Perry must open the Japanese ports even if
he has to open his owm." (44)
For the firet time in their history the Sewurai warriors and their great
swords were impotent. The Japanese had their choice of opening their ports or
having them blowm to bite by Perry's 32-pounders.
There was consternetion among the Japanese. Without e solution to their
own intornal problems, all factions hurriedly agreed resistance wes futile. The
Americans concluded a treaty securing open ports.
Commodore Perry mms magnenimous in his promises: "If the Jepanese camo
to the Unitod States, they would find the navigable weters of the country free
to them, and that thoy would not be debarred even from the gold flelds of
California." (45)
Perry's treaty presented  new problom or Japan. So long ss she prac-
ticed rigid isolation, national unity ms rather inconsequential. But now that
she wmas to deel with foreign powers she must present a united front. Each rival
faction was determined not to give in to the othar. In the end there was only
one solution, self-evidont from t first: the restoration of the Emperor.
The stage mas set for this restoration. The one thing ell rivel clans
had in common was loyalty to the Emperor. Only the Emperor could represent Japan
and ratify treaties with Westerners. The revival of learming of the Samurai
period had engendered a new mave of patriotism. Clan spirit fused into national
pride. For the Restoration of the Samurai, loyal to the last, gave up their
entire incomes from their old clans, without a murmur. (46)
During the reorganization incident to the Restoretion, the one thing
essential to Japan wms peace. She hed no national army. In 1877 Japan was forced
to make certain concessions to Koree. Enraged by this spineless action, the
Satsume olan broke out into open rovolt against the newly organised government.
It was the final plee for e return to the old order. The rebellion was bitterly
fought and the central government was foroed to resort to the use of something
novel in Japanese history - a brand new national army of consoripts, composed
mot of Samurai gentry but largely of stook from the lowly walks of life.

CONFIDENTIAL
M E M O R A N D U M
January 12, 1940.
Subject:
: Statements made by Mr. Obata, of the
American Section of the Japanese
Foreign Office, to Mr. Abend.
Following is a copy of Mr. Abend's record of statements
made to him by Mr. Obata.
"Off the Record memos of conversation Saturday, January
6th when Mr. Obata of the American Section of the Tokyo
Foreign Office, called upon me for third time during his
trip to China.
'I'm flying back to Tokyo Tuesday morning, and I'm pretty
disheartened. The loud-mouthed and violent-acting section
of the Army is still in control. While it is true that they
are a minority - even an Army minority - they are the direct
actionists' of Japan and therefore have a power entirely
unjustified by their numbers.'
'The Japanese moderates do not dare to advocate nor to
attempt to carry out policies too much in opposition to this
violent and vocal group, for if we did so there might easily
be another February uprising in Tokyo. And that would finish
moderation in Japan for a long time, and would probably
finish Japan as a world power too. Peoples and governments
abroad do not realize how this group always holds the sword
of threatened violence over our necks.'
'Today our Foreign Office is all for moderation and for
genuine and sincere appeasement of the democracies. But the
Foreign Office is weak. Nomura is weak because he opposes
the extremists. The only encouraging sign I can see is the
fact that at last our most important Army leaders realize
001
that we have gone as far as we can - farther than we should
have gone - in China. But they cannot call a halt either.
N.k..beab
We cannot resort to a Stalinist kind of purge in our Army.
That would only increase our difficulties, and would augument
the strength of the violent-acting group in the military.'
"We then discussed the plans for the Wang Ching-wei
'Central Government' and I told Obata I thought they were
running the whole show wrong, You give the poor man no
chance to gain prestige nor make face with his own people,
I advised. The whole thing is being done in Tokyo, con-
sidered in manifold meetings in Tokyo, approved in Tokyo.
Surely your China group recognizes the necessity for face over
here. The whole thing should have been made to seem to have
been arranged and hatched here in Shanghai or in Nanking or
in Tsingtao. To please a small group of self-important
Japanese you are grinding the word 'puppet' ever more deeply
across Wang's brow."
"Obata

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
Obata agreed, and then wanted to know what I thought
Ign repercussions would be. Almost nil, except a
asure of scornful disapproval, I told him." And then I
dded that I was particularly sorry to see this puppet
regime founded because when Japan recognizes this creation,
when she exchanges Ambassadors, she will have committed
herself irrevocably. She could never afford to jeopardize
her national honor by later disavowing her own creation to
appease third powers. To me a recovery of American-Japanese
cordiality was the most important issue involved in this
Wang Ching-wei scheme. I iid not see how the United States,
or any of the other Nine Power Treaty Powers, could ever
recognize such a creation, negotiate with it over trade or
other matters or concede to Japan the legality of such a
step."
"Obata was very thoughtful. 'I'll do my best to arrange
behind the scenes for a breakdown of the parleys Wang Ching-
wei is to hold with the Nanking and Peking leaders at Tsing-
tao. If the thing seemed to break down because of Chinese
inability to agree, then Japan would not lose face in China
nor abroad - nor at home either.'
"When he left my apartment he walked right across the
hall to the apartment occupied by Ambassador Kato to ask
Kato to cable in code to Tokyo urging an abandonment of the
Wang Ching-wei plan."
"Obata said he felt the whole Wang Ching-wei affair
had been mishandled from the start end I agreed. Said he:
Wang should have remained in Hanoi or Haiphong until
a semblance of popular clamor for his coming to the occupied
areas had been arranged for. Instead he came here, nego-
tiated with the Japanese and then went to Japan. All of
which was bad for his prestige in China and abroad. I
think we'd better drop the whole plan, on the pretext that
Chinese harmony could not be achieved and say we'll await
the advent of a stronger leader before again pledging
Japanese support.'
"Just before he left Obata made the most amazing propo-
sition:
'Abend I wish you'd come to Japan -- just for a day or
two if that is all the time you could spare. When I get
back I'll arrange a secret meeting with Prince Konoye, the
Foreign Minister, Premier Abe, General Hata, Matsuoka and
a few others. Please come and tell them frankly what you
think of our whole show over here. They will be glad to
listen and if you can help to influence an abandonment of
the Wang Ching-wei affair you will have done a great service
to your own country and to mine. For I see clearly now that
if we recognize Wang Ching-wei we'll have burned our bridges
so far as a return to American-Japanese friendly relations
is concerned.'
"I told him I could not commit myself. That I had no
license to interfere in diplomacy. That we was free to tell
his people in Tokyo the substance of our talks. But he
pressed the point that a face to face talk with Japan's leaders
would be of much more convincing value - to them and to me.
He will. cable me if he arranges such a meeting and then I'll
ask the Consul General or Admiral Glassford to send THE TIMES
a coded inquiry as to whether I should make such a trip."
off

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Off the Record memo on last conversation with Obata,
who spent an hour with me late afternoon of January 8th
and flew to Tokyo on January 9th.
"When Obata first flew over here from Tokyo about a
fortnight ago, we discussed many things including the
Tientsin situation. He wanted to know if I thought
Britain would ever forgive that 'inexcusable blockade'.
I countered by asking him if Japan would ever forgive
it if British soldiers in London or liverpool stripped
Japanese men and gentle Japanese women, threw their
clothes in the street and made them dress in front of
grinning hundreds of the British equivalents to coolies.
I also asked him if Japan would ever forgive a government
supporting a general who blockaded a Japanese occupied
area in Britain, in peace time, and let babies die in
the heat - denying them milk, making it compulsory to
open all milk bottles going into the blockaded area,
stirring the contents vith dirty sticks, under the pretense
the milk bottle might contain a bomb. Ánd then letting
the milk sit in the July-August sun until it soured."
"Obata squirmed. 'But our government in Tokyo never
sanctioned those measures' he parried. "But General
Homma who ordered them is still in command at Tientsin,"
I replied. 'What can we do? what should we do,' said
Obata nervously. "Any other self-respecting government
with real authority over its military would recall, degrade
and retire such a general," I told him.
'We dare not.
It would mean a rebellion - a military coup and diotator-
ship.'
"Obata told me he had gone to Peking and Tientsin
by air and had investigated the General Homma mess." 'You
must believe me, Mr. Abend,' he said seemingly sincerely
but maybe as a whitewash, 'that General Homma is a fine
man. He is even pro-British. One of his sons is being
educated in England. He himself was military attache in
London, likes England, likes Britishers. But he is
powerless against his ruffianly younger officers. They
have forced all this and have forced the anti-British
campaign in North China. They may force an anti-American
campaign after January 26th. Homma himself told me he
feels personally disgraced but like many of our generals
he is powerless.
"Obata says the life of the Abe cabinet is nearly
ended. He fears an accession to power by the most ignor-
ant and most ruthless wing of the Army."
E. F. Stanton

Reaignation
Tokyo's 4486, February 13, 1940, Abelabinet.
General Abe was a novice in overnment
and politics, as became clear as soon as he
became premier at the end of Au ust 1939.
Economic strain in Japan was not adequately
met by his policy of procrastination. Abe's
decision to overlook criticism from the Diet
was unfortunate. On January 8, 1940 things
came to a head when the Minister of War in-
formed the oremier thet the Army was no
longer in position to support the Government.
In the preliminary maneuvers before the
formation of the new cabinet an effort was
apparently made to induce the Army to assume
complete responsibility for civil as well as
military administration under the new cebinet.
This the Army refused to do. At this stage,
according to the Embassy, the Emperor's
advisers took an unprecedented step: the
War Minister was summoned to the Palace
where he was told by the Emperor, it ap ears,
that the Army must abandon its obstructive
tactics and cooperate with whatever cabinet
was formed. However, the person selected
to form a cabinet (Admir l Yonai) was the
one remaining candidate who had the anrrovrl
of the Army.
While

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
While the new cabinet has no outstanding
statesmen, all of its members are men of long
experience in official life. The premier is
a man whose greatest asset, apart from great
courage, probably lies in his friendship with
Prince Konoe.
The Cabinet's policies ap ear to be no
different from those of previous cabinets
which have come into being since the outbreak
of the hostilities in China.

Supplementing my telegrams No. 713, December 28,
1939, 7 p.m.; No. 12, January 11, 1940, noon; No. 17,
January 12, 6 p.m.; No. 18, January 14, noon; No. 20,

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
Mitsumasa Yonai, of a new Cabinet, I have the honor to
relate in detail the course of political developments
revolving around General Abe's resignation, the selec-
tion of his successor, and the formation of the new
Cabinet.
General Abe, it will be recalled, was a novice in
government and in politics, and it beoame apparent soon
after his investiture in the closing days of August, l939,
that the life of his Cabinet would be short at best.
Hardly a month had passed before the new Prime Minister,
by the enactment of a Cabinet decree empowering him to
issue at will wartime emergency orders to his various
Cabinet officers, incurred the bitter resentment of the
Privy Council which cast doubts first of all on the con-
stitutionality of the new measure and declared that in
any event its members should have been afforded the oppor-
tunity of prior consultation before action was taken on
measures of such nature.
The first clear indication of the Abe Cabinet's
weakness came in the abandonment of the so-called small
cabinet principle which had been adopted to avoid the
dangers of an imperium in imperio so damaging to the Konoe
and Hiranuma Cabinets and in which a large part of the
strength of the new Cabinet presumably lay. The immediate
cause for this step lay in General Abe's failure to settle
a series of troublesome disputes which had arisen over
questions of jurisdiction between various Ministries of
the Government, with the result that most of the port-
folios held concurrently had to be filled in relatively
short

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Bhort order, leaving the Japanese public with a poor im-
pression of General Abe's political skill.
Again, in October, as a result of the Cabinet's de-
cision to set up a Trade Ministry which would in fact have
relieved the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of a large part
of its duties, a decision generally regarded as hastily
considered if not ill-conceived, open revolt took place
in the Foreign Office culminating in the tendering of
resignations by a large and important portion of the For-
eign Office personnel. Faced with a situation which would
virtually paralyze the administration of one of the most
important branches of the Government, the Prime Minister
entrusted the settlement of the dispute to the Foreign
Minister, Admiral Nomura, and through his efforts the
resignations were withdrawn, the Government on its part
issuing a statement absolving all participants from blame.
While the Abe Cabinet had again weathered the storm, there
was little doubt that it had been shaken badly and that
the confidence reposed in it by the people had been under-
mined to a degree which would seriously affect its ability
to deal with future crises.
Meanwhile Japan's internal economic situation showed
signs of wearing thin. The problems of price control and
distribution of commodities were, it is true, legacies
from the Konoe and Hiranuma Cabinets, with this important
differenoe: Japan's internal economic situation had changed
rapidly during the ensuing months. By the time the Abe
Cabinet came on the scene, danger signals pointing toward
inflation had already made their appearance. While the
Abe Cabinet put into effect in October a series of so-
called price checking ordinances long under consideration,
1t

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
it adhered in the main to the policy of drift which had
charaoterized its predecessors. Moreover, in November,
the Government by suddenly raising the price of cigarettes,
the manufacture and sale of which is a Government monopoly,
brought down upon its head a storm of disapproval from the
press, the accusation being made that the Government had
not only reversed completely its declared polioy of main-
taining low prices but had discriminated against private
as opposed to Government enterprise. Finally, there were,
during the closing months of l939, definite signs that the
measures taken by the Government to curb inflation and to
correct the maldistribution of essential commodities,
notably rice, were wholly inadequate, and grumbling against
the Government's policy of drift became increasingly co-
herent and articulate. Efforts on the part of the Prime
Minister to bring the leader of the Minseito party into
the Cabinet ended in failure and only served to convince
the public that not only was the Cabinet dubious about
its own position, but that the Minseito did not choose to
be associated with a Government which needed support in
the Diet to keep on its feet.
The main current of social unrest which had remained
hidden up to this time now manifested itself openly in
the presentation on December 27, 1939, of a petition signed
by some 250 members of the Diet asking Premier Abe to re-
sign, to which the Prime Minister boldly replied in a
public statement several days later that he would not be
deterred by internal difficulties but would proceed with
the accomplishment of the principal objective of his Cabinet,
the settlement of the China incident. The Premier had been
onoouraged

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.98)
-5
encouraged in his determination to stay, it now seems
clear, by the inability of the statesmen around the Throne
to conceive of a successor who could unite all the political
forces in the country and who was possessed at the same
time of the determination to carry through the measures
necessary to solve Japan's internal and external difficul-
ties. Before long, however, it was evident that the action
of the 250 Diet members was more than an isolated political
gesture and that if General Abe was determined to remain
he would face a vote of non-confidence in the Diet and
eventually the problem of dissolution of that body or his
own resignation. While the year-end gave the Cabinet a
breathing spell, the bandying about of names in the press
of a likely successor was eloquent prophecy that General
Abe's days as Premier were numbered.
The initial step in reaching a solution of the rapidly
developing political crisis came, it appears, from the
Army, General Hata, the Minister of War, having informed
the Premier on January 8, 1940, that the Army was no longer
in position to support the Government, at which point there
began the traditional political maneuvers of the men around
the Throne. The Army's first choice, it appears, was Prince
Fumimaro Konoe, the President of the Privy Council. Not
only was Prince Konoe one of the most powerful of Japan's
non-political and non-military statesmen, but he was asso-
ciated in the popular mind with the historic statement in
respect of Japan's policy toward China which bore his name.
Despite repeated urging, Prince Konoe flatly refused the
Army's request, and in conjunction with Mr. Kurahei Yuasa,
Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal, laid before the War Minister
the suggestion that a General on the active list, preferably
J
General

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
-6
General Hata himself, accept the Imperial command to
form a new Government. It was clear that one of the most
significant contests in contemporary Japanese political
history was taking place and that the Emperor's advisers
were determined to place the full responsibility for ad-
ministration upon the Army, which had consistently avoided
such responsibility but had persisted in its efforts to
control the Government. At a council of high military
officials on January 14, 1940, it was decided that the
acceptance by General Hata or any other military officer
of the premiership under the conditions laid down by Prince
Konoe and Mr. Yuasa could only mean that the Army would be
forced to assume complete responsibility for civil as well
as military administration, a step which the Army felt it
could not properly take.
Accordingly, the only remaining candidate who had the
approval of the Army was Admiral Mitsumasa Yonai, who had
commended himself to the Emperor's advisers originally by
reason of his strength of character, moderateness of views,
and the fact that he had become thoroughly conversant with
the problems facing Japan during his two years' service
as Navy Minister in the Konoe and Hiranuma Cabinets. The
Emperor's advisers then took an unprededented step: the
War Minister was summoned to the Palace where he was told
by the Emperor, it appears, that the Army must abandon its
obstructive tactics and cooperate with whatever Cabinet
was formed. After the nominal approval of the Genro, Prince
Saionji, had been obtained, Admiral Yonai received on Janu-
ary 14 the Imperial command to form a new Cabinet. The Abe
Cabinet had remained in office less than five months, its
life being the second shortest in Japanese political history.
Within

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.95)
-7-
Within slightly more than twenty-four hours of his
.acceptance of the Imperial command, Admiral Yonai had
completed the selection of the new Cabinet composed of the
following:
Prime Minister: Admiral Mitsumasa Yonai
00F.
Minister for Foreign Affairs: Mr. Hachiro Arita
Minister of Home Affairs: Count Hideo Kodama
Minister of Finance: Mr. Yukio Sakurauchi
Minister of War: General Shunroku Hata
00
Minister of Navy: Vice-Admiral Zengo Yoshida
Minister of Justice: Mr. Shotatsu Kimura
Minister of Education: Mr. Chinjiro Matsuura
Minister of Agriculture and Forestry: Mr. Toshio Shimada
(P)
Minister of Commerce and Industry: Mr. Ginjiro Fujihara
Minister of Communications: Mr. Masanori Katsu
NOj
Minister of Railways: Mr. Tsuruhei Matsuno
Minister of Overseas Affairs: General Kuniaki Koiso
Minister of Welfare: Mr. Shigeru Yoshida
Chief Secretary of the Cabinet: Mr. Sotaro Ishiwata
Director of the Legislative Bureau of the Cabinet:
Mr. Hisatada Hirose
Director of the Cabinet Planning Board: Mr. Kakichi
Takeuchi
AA
While the new Cabinet has no outstanding statesmen,
all of its members are men with long erperience in official
life. The Minister of War, General Shunroku Hata, and the
Minister of Navy Vice-Admiral Zengo Yoshida, are "holdovers*
from the old Cabinet, while the new Minister of Finance,
Mr. Yukio Sakurauohi, was Minister of Agriculture and
Forestry

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
Forestry under the Hiranuma Cabinet. Aside from Mr.
Sakurauchi's familiarity with the problems of a "controlled"
economy, he brings to the Cabinet the views of a success-
ful businessman and politician. Moreover, his appointment
must be regarded as somewhat of a departure, as recent
holders of the Finance Ministry have not been party men.
It is obvious from his anteoedents that it was not his
limited experience in finance which brought about his ap-
pointment to this post. The Minister for Foreign Affairs,
Mr. Hachiro Arita, is no newcomer to that Ministry, having
held the portfolio in the Konoe and Hiranuma Cabinets.
The new Minister of Commerce and Industry, Mr. Ginjiro
Fujihara, a businessman and long an exponent of "laissez
faire" capitalism, is making his initial appearance as a
government official. Mr. Fujihara's intimate friendship
with the Minister of Finance is expected to facilitate the
important economic liaison work between that Ministry and
his own. Moreover, industry looks to Mr. Fujihara to
mitigate the harshness of control measures. While the new
Minister of Communications, Mr. Masanori Katsu, is styled
by the press as the representative in the Cabinet of the
Minseito party, he is better known as a bureaucrat, having
served for a long period in the Ministry of Finance. The
Minister of Home Affairs, Count Hideo Kodama, a brother-
K0
in-law of General Terauchi and Marquis Kido, has a long
20181
record of public service, having served most recently as
Minister of Communications in the Hayashi Cabinet. Out-
standing members, but not leaders of their respective
factions, of the Seiyukai are Mr. Tsuruhei Matsuno (Kuhara),
Minister of Railways, and Mr. Toshio Shimada (Nakajima),
Minister of Agriculture and Forestry. Both the Ministry
of Justice and the Ministry of Education are headed by
professional

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
professional men of almost complete obscurity in offictal
life but prominent in their respective fields. Mr. Shigeru
Yoshida, the Minister of Welfare, is a bureaucrat by pro-
fession and reputedly a close student of social welfare
problems. The new Minister of Overseas Affairs, General
Kuniaki Koiso, holder of the same portfolio in the Hiranuma
Cabinet, will be remembered as one of the most outspoken
of the politically-minded group in the Army and a consistent
advocate of administrative reform. The new Chief Secretary
of the Cabinet, Mr. Sotaro Ishiwata, who with the Directors
of the Legislative Bureau and the Cabinet Planning Board
enjoys a position closely approximating Cabinet rank, is
a close personal friend of Admiral Yonai's as well as having
been his intimate as Finance Minister in the Hiranuma Cabinet.
It is generally expected that the Cabinet will look to Mr.
Ishiwata rather than to Mr. Sakurauchi for guidance in
matters financial, the complexity and importance of which
grow daily.
It will have been observed that the Yonai Cabinet is
a congeries of diverse and discordant elements: party men
without party responsibility, businessmen, a soldier-
politician, academicians, bureaucrats, and a landed aris-
tocrat. It is headed by a man whose greatest asset -- apart
from great courage -- probably lies in his friendship with
the President of the Privy Council, Prince Fumimaro Konoe,
and the high esteem in which he is held by Mr. Kurahei
Yuasa, the Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal, although he has
won for himself while serving in the Hiranuma and Konoe
Cabinets an excellent reputation for administrative ability
and frankness.
The

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
The circumstances in which the present Cabinet was
brought into being make it a transitional one. The Yonai
Cabinet follows a series of governments which have pur-
sued policies, both internal and external, the effects of
which have been destructive without the achieving of any
benefits which can be regarded in any way as real or per-
manent. Opinion in influential quarters, as well as among
the masses, has clearly revealed, by the turning out of
the Abe Cabinet, that it will no longer tolerate a progressive
deterioration of national economy, with constantly increas-
ing hardships in the way of rising prices and scarcity of
commodities, without there being increasing prospects of
progress toward liquidation of the conflict with China.
When notice was served on General Abe that unparalleled
capacity for procrastination would no longer suffice to
keep him in office, clearly the time had come for construc-
tive effort and for a leader capable not only of commanding
the support of the different political elements but of
taking initiative along constructive lines. Whether such
a leader has been found in Admiral Yonai remains to be
seen, but there is one thing certain, and that is, thanks
to the Emperor's personal advisers, Admiral Yonai begins
his task with perceptible indioation of willingness on the
part of the Army to give the new Government an opportunity
to try its hand at constructive effort -- within the frame-
work, however, of the continental policy.
V
The contribution of the Emperor's personal advisers
for turning national attention in the direction of modera-
tion and reason is, in my view, a development of sufficient
importance to merit more than passing mention. Their
movements before and during the Cabinet crisis are still
obsoure, but it appears to be a fact that these experienced
statesmen

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
statesmen proposed in the first instance that the Army
take over the prime ministership; and the assumption is
both reasonable and permissible that in so doing they
confronted the Army with a choice either of assuming the
responsibility for bringing order out of the existing
confusion or of giving the Government more latitude than
it has in the past. The Army tried to avoid being hung
on either horn of the dilemma by forbidding any officer
on the active list to accept the prime ministership, but
the taking of this position served only to manifest the
Army's lack of confidence in its ability to construct
anything lasting and permanent out of the disorders result-
ing from the implementation of the Army's own policies.
In the opinion of many Japanese observers, the Army cannot
now afford to hold the Government on too short a rein and
the time has now come for it to give those with intelligent
knowledge of international problems, of economics and of
finance, more latitude in the working out of problems with
which the Army is unwilling or unable to cope. This expecta-
tion is, of course, conceived by knowledge that the continued
uncompromising pursuit of the objectives of the conflict in
China as originally formulated can lead eventually only to
placing the national interests completely and unreservedly
in the hands of the Army -- a certain guarantee of political
and social changes of a far-reaching character, if not of
worse.
In so far as the Cabinet's policies are concerned, they
appear to be no different from those of previous Cabinets
which have come into being since the outbreak of the China
ineident. It may be surmised from public statements of the
Prime Minister and other officers of the Cabinet that the
polieies of the Abe Cabinet with respect to domestic affairs,
tho

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.98)
-12-
the expansion of produotive power, the promotion of foreign
trade, and the security of livelihood of the people, will
be continued with greater emphasis laid on more rigid methods
of economic control. In the realm of foreign affairs, no
substantial change in Japan's announced policy of non-
involvement in Europe and adjustment of relations with
Great Britain, the United States, and the Soviet Union
would appear to be in the offing.
As we see it, the principal mission of the Yonai Gov-
ernment is to test the national temper and to determine
the direction toward which the nation proposes to proceed.
Respectfully yours,
.
Joseph C. Grew.

DEPARTMENT OFSTATE
May 23, 1940
Osaka's April 13, 1940,"Activities of
Mr. Tei-ichi Muto and the Organization 'Sai-
sei".
The Consul at Osaka herein makes an inter-
esting report of a movement, which he believes
may or may not assume national proportions, now
centered in the Osaka area and devoted to
fascist ideas. The principles of the movement
are (1) unification of government and religion
on the basis of a strong nationalistic Shinto
faith, (2) a strong central government, (3)
support of Germany and opposition to Great
Britain, and (4) anti-Jewish activities. The
organization is called "Sai-sei" and has a
monthly publication in which Mr. Tei-ichi Muto,
former columnist on the Osaka Asahi and now
assistant editor of the Osaka Jiji, is referred
to by the title shu-mei, a term having more or
less the connotation of the title used by
Hitler. Mr. Muto devotes a great deal of time
to lecturing and to writing books which carry
out the principles of the movement.
In Osaka's despatch of April 27, 1940,
"Interest Displayed in American Activities by
Local Press", it will be recalled that the
Osaka Jiji, of which Mr. Muto is assistant
editor, 1s reported to favor definite action
by Japan to settle once and for all the ques-
tion of the disposal of the Netherlands Indies
even should it involve serious trouble with
the United States.

Mr. Welles.
dee tel, 613 fron, 2olayo July 23 6 foem
I call attention especially to paragraph four of
F.W..
Mr. Grew's telegram, a copy of which is here attached.
The "opinion" of which an account is given in this
894.00/934
paragraph seems to me to be based on sound considerations.
Especially sound, in my view, is the reference to the
potentially effective influence of "economic consid-
erations". In that connection, the fact that there
exists and there hangs over the head of the Japanese
leaders the possibility of substantial embargoes (both
in the export field and in the import field) by the
United States is of great importance. But the possi-
bility of substantial embargoes by this country is linked
closely with the question of the existing location and
the possible alteration of the location of the U. S.
Battle Fleet. So long as we have the Fleet in the
Pacific, and especially while we have it at Hawaii, the
Japanese have reason to fear what we may do in the field
of embargoes. The Fleet stands as the defensive and
shielding arm behind which and screened by which there
is
F
N

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.98)
121327
-2-
is the potential striking power of the other arm,
embargo. If and when our Fleet leaves the Pacific and
moves into the Atlantic, Japan will have reason to be
very little fearful that we will go in extensively for
embargoes. Thus, our present setup--with authority for
embargoes and with the Fleet in the Pacific--contributes
greatly to the maintenance of the negative equilibrium
in Japanese political thought which is described in the
paragraph under reference and should contribute toward
making good the "best opinion today" to the effect "that
there will be no abrupt and drastic change in [Japan's]
foreign policy in the immediate future."
八
Stanley K. Hornbeck

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 9, 1940
Harbin's despatch no. 262, May 9, 1940,
"Conversation Between Two Japanese Regarding
Conditions At Home And On The Continent".
In this conversation, overhead by Vice
Consul Johansen, two Japanese indicated that
they believed that the development of Man-
churia is handicapped and perhaps doomed to
failure by the lack of trained and able men
and that many policies were being put into
effect in this area which should still be
in the discussion stage. The Japanese also
questioned the advisability of antagonizing
Britain and America and expressed concern
over the future of their country.

MEMORANDUM
Subject: Convers tion between two Japanese
regarding conditions at home and
on the continent.
while returning iroa eipin on May ,  over-
heard a conversation between two Japanese who wore
sharing the train compart ..ent with me and my wife.
The conversation seems interestin: as a "straw in
the wind" inuicating the o Inion and feeling of
educated classes of Jananese with respect to the
activities of those who rule them.
One of the speakers had the appearance of a
scholar or artist, and the other Identified himself
at one point as a tecnnical expert (gijitsuka).
The frankness wit which they expressei them-
selves was especially striking in view of the fact
that they obviously had not met before snd that part
of the conversation tock place after I had spoken to
them in Japanese on trivial subjects at sufficient
length for them to know that I would be able to fol-
low their conversation shoula 1 try.
The conversation nay be sunned up as an expression
of concern, at times in impassioned terms by the scholar
or artist, over the future of their country and its ven-
tures in Manchuria and North China. I recall the fol-
lowing specific points:
1. The development of Manchuria is handicapped
and perhaps doomed to failure because of t he lack of
trained and able men. A wealth of natural resources
is of little practical value without a wealth of men
and talent, - the most important element for the suc-
cess of a nation. The technician, who was or had been
working in "Manchukuo", nentioned his own diificulties
on that score.
2. In "Manchukuo" many policies are put into ef-
fect which should still be in the discussion stage.
For example, far-reaching agricultural programs had
been put into operation before they had been thought
out. In addition, projects are handicapped by the
deficiency of personnel.
3. What 1s the use of antagonizing countries like
America and England? Signs like those in Peiping ( I
think he referred to anti-British signs) are a mistake.
- 4. -

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.93)
- 2 -
4. Japan is a poor propagandist. he has uch
to learn of this and other mattors from  estern
countrtes.
5. It wes ridiculous to me ke su n ise of
the saito case in the  jet.
6. The speakere seid many times that if thinge
contimed in their present fashion Japan could not
progress (hattatsu). It wes when they mentioned the
situation as a whole, that the note of literally
passionete concern gripped the voiee of the scholar
or artist.
oppo T. Johanson
American conevlate Gen aral.
Harbin, y 7, 1940.

Dear Mr. Rogers:
Right now the attentions of everyone out here are riveted on
894.0
the tragic developments in urone, and for my part, find it herd
indeed to bring back thoughts to study and the lesser nroblems and
events close at hand. All the same, things have been happening in
Japan as well, and the recent economic decisions to ration and
check profits look particularly far-reaching. o give you a clearer
idea of what hes led to these, 'll bring down to date my hard times
report of a few months ago.
8
6
According to the findings of the various business survey grouns,
4
until this last month there has been a fairly constant rise in the
cost of living in urban areas - around two percent per month for
0
the man of average modest means, and considerably more for consumers
O
of luxury goods. In April, however, the indey took a six nercent
spurt instead of the norrai two, with food prices hiking the highest.
6
In itself this change is not considered serious, but there are fears
5
that it may point to a speedy end to low prices, with all the incident
5
dangers.
Changes, that is to say increas s, in the nrices of goods subject
to the control of the ent al rice Policy  ommittee have been
sanctioned in such nunbers that the so-celled nrice pegging" policy
ap ears to be in reality more of a brake than a check. Likewi e,
infractions of these control regulations are being reported on a scale
hich indicates a wide-spread wil ingness to dispense ith governmentel
aid in bergaining for the scarcer commodities. As the  inister of
Tustice vointed out in a recent speech, the curbing of such illicit
transaetions now is the chief problem before Japan's law administration
agencies.
This section of the country sot a break in a mild winter, for had
the season been normal, the suffering from fuel shortages would heve
been much more intense. On the other hand, certein necessities have
become distressingly scarce. I don't say that one who knew where and
how to look for leather would have had my lack of success, but after
two mcnths of hunting for a place which could make a pair of skate
FILHD
shoes, I finally believed what I read in the newspapers and gave up.
The butter supply has beon low, and at times okyo retailers have
enforced a rationing system emong regular customers. Coffee stores
still traffic in "blends" into whose composition one would hate to
inquire, but the rule of one pound to the customer, plus & limit 
the store's total daily sales makes for early and freouent shopning.
Furthermore, quite often "out" signs are seen of evenings in cafes,
when the day's grounds have ben boiled nast all further decomposition.
In the interest of decreased consumption the closing time for beer-halls
and bars has been advanced from twelve to eleven, but nevertheless
the customers are leaving with unslaked thirsts in the "later" hours
of the evening. A table d'hote dinner at the Imperial Hotel now costs
a third more than when I was there in October.

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.99)
40
The commodities hit the hardest, though, are matches and sugar.
All winter long, one match run followed another, and for weeks at a
time literally not one could be bought. The Japanese equivalent of
"Got a light, Buddy " reputedly never was in greater use, and at
times was cracking down all reserve on the streets end in cefes:
foreigners, for their part, were finding the old three-on-a-match
superstition out of pace with the times. s for sugar, after buying
and mixing all we could get of brown, chunk, and what-have-you, my
family still has had to go light and aprly the test "If you can taste
it in the coffee, vou heve taken too much." Nor hevethe domestic
users alone been up against it, for the manager of one of the largest
foreign restaurants recently confided to me that he fills his bowls
only by turning out all his cooks and waiters early each morning to
scour the town.
However, these days of invigorating uncertainty and freedom to
score (and then brag of) occasional shopning successes are numbered.
From the 5th of June there begins a complete rationing of sugar,
matches, and (in the rural districts) cotton articles. Adult or
child, foreigner or Japanese, each month each person may purchase but
one half pound of sugar and two small boxes of metches, containing
perhaps a hundred or a hundred and fifty in all. The rationing of
other articles likely will follow when the machinery of distribution
for purchase tickets gets functioning smoothly, the authorities say.
In view of the shortages and runs, rationing is the fair and
sensible way to equalize discomfort, as I think we all agree, although
there is sone doubt of the plan to cut Tokyo's su"ply in favor of
rural districts, and a basis of distribution which favors the man
with fifteen children. Hone is expressed in the newspapers that
quantities soon can be increased, but I have a hunch that my embassy
friends - basking in their diplomatic immunities - will see a lot
more of me hereafter, along about dinner time.
The other important measure, already in effect, is the Army's
regulation of nrofits t ken by all concerns engaged in any stage of
the manufacture of military supplies. The volume of army purchases
alone is enough to affect a substantial block of the nation's business,
but in addition the civil government now is considering following suit
by supervising all other forms of business not reached by the Army.
hat all this actually amounts to, no one quite knows, or is telling.
An "appropriate" profit for all is a laudable objective, but whether
it will be a constant for all business or for only certain classes,
or whether it will be left to the discretion of individuals to make
allowance for such factors as need of expansion, ohligations, etc.
remains to be seen. Opinion has it that the profiie ers in the munitions
industries are the people really drawing the fire.
"Where Man leaves off, Nature begins" is the philosonhical keynote
to many a conversation these days, for in addition to what I have
described, Tokyo has on hand a water shortage of the gravest nature.
The snowless winter, coupled with last year's subnormal rainy season,
has the dust flying in the river and reservoir beds. For several
weeks five of Tokyo's wards have gotten no water at all through the
mains, and their residents have existed only by buying from street
venders. Naturally the fire menace in these sections is frightful,
and the "flower of Yedo" could blossom again as luxuriantly and as
disastrously as in the old days.

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.96)
3 - 40
My part of town has been relatively well off, for while for
months we have not always had water in the daytime, by leaving the
tap onen of an evening we could catch enough in the bath tub to
suffice. Now that srout lso has dried un, and it takes a before-
brea'fast bucket line to a nearby hydrent to keen the tub filled.
f the pressure goes still lower bofore th rairs come in about a
month, my summer exodus will take place in edintoly, regardless of
temperature or the uncertainty of dest ination.
so keep your eve on the address of these letters - that will
tel the whole story.

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Tokyo, Japan
June 1st,1940
Dear Mr. Rogers:
Df Sate
No one of the four hundred will find his position the more
precarious when news of this gets around, but be that as it may,
for a while this week I traveled in high society - to be exact,
about ten minutes, which was the length of my chat with Prince
Chichibu, brother of the Emperor of Japan.
The occasion was a dinner of the cambridge-Ox ord Society,
an alumni organization of Japanese and English. Soon after coming
out here, I was put up by an acquaintance at the british Embaasy,
and when the club's membership requirement afterward was relaxed
in favor of Japanese students forced home by the war, I was slinned
in under the ropes, Ever since, this group's monthly luncheon,
with its mixed crowd of businessmen and diplomats, has been one of
the bright spots, and through contacts there I have come to know
about as many English as Americans.
Recently I got a notice of the annual dinner where such guests
as Admiral Hiraga, President of Tokyo Imperial University, Viscount
atsudaira, and Sir Robert Cragie, the British Ambassador were
expected, and the further recital that "Ilis Imperial Highness,
Prince Chichibu has graciously signified his intention of honoring
the society with his presence." Nothing more was promised than
a chance to gawk, but on arriving I found that an after-dinner
informal presentation was in store for each new member.
His Highness is a member of the society in his own right, for
he went up at Oxford in 1925, although I don't think that he
remained for the full three years. hile he never comes to the
usual gatherings, he always turns up at the dinners - a fact which
heartens the British Embassy crowd no end, for they say it is purely
voluntary, and regard it as a distinct concession. Usually his
appearances are clcsely circumscribed, even though he does represent
the Imperial House publicly much more often than his brother, who
comes out of seclusion only a few times each year on occasions of
the highest importance. To judge from pictures, there is a close
resemblance.
As is proper for the honor guest, he arrived last and left
first. Both times he made the rounds of the group for personal
greetings, shaking hands with the foreigners and exchanging bows
with the Japanese - that is to say, giving the normal kind and
getting in return something near a salaam.
In my turn after dinner, I found him quite skilled in setting
one at ease, and a speaker of fairly good English. He took the
initiative through most of the conversation, professing an interest
in my work and pressing me as to details. We also got onto the

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
41
the subjects of summer vacations and the present housing shortage
in Tokyo, and the inevitable topic of the lack of water, where I
choked off with some effort the stock questions about conservatior.
measures being taken at home.
The only ticklish moment came when he started to switch the
conversation into Japanese. The plane of speech to members of
the Royal Blood is a good three stories above the most nolite and
involved I have ever had contact with - renutedly e poser even for
educated Japanese, and I well knew it, so after a simnle reply or
so I came back into English where we stuck it out in safety for
the rest of the time.
Technically this probably was a usurpation of His ighness's
prerogative, particularly inasmuch as I had declared that  was
a language student, although if he so considered it he was much too
courteous to let it show. I could hav made a few simple and
understandable observations, I suppose, end surely he would have
over-looked all shortcomings, but this was something I preferred
not to do. Quite a while ago I thought it over and concluded that
for some distance yet the best policy for me is to evoid the use
of Japanese in important convorsations.
I remember quite well Ambassador Grew's remarks along this
line one evening during our crossing. i Japanese in our group had
asked if he spoke Japanese, and Mr. Gre renlied that although he
had lived in Japan for a number of years (actually he is the doven
of the diplomatic community) he spoke none at all. "hen I came
here," he went on to explain "I ws told that to learn to speak would
require two years of study, which I did not have to give. And rather
than speak Japanese badly, I decided not to try at all." After that
he recounted some of the misadventures of his colleagues who had
not been as prudent.
This attitude appears to be most sensible, especially for a
man of Mr. Grew's standing whose statements always must be guarded.
And the farther I go with Jananese, the better I can tell that there
is especial need for such caution here, for the onnortunities to be
ambiguous appear practically infinite. f course, my status is far
different from a high-ranking diplomat's end also I am doing language
work, but just the same I am going slowly. I don't mean by that
that I talk nothing but English, for naturally I take every chance
of practicing, even to the extent I feer of imposing sometimes pon
cab drivers, clerks, policemen and the like. But at times when a
conversation means something, when an idea must be gotten over without
risk of mistake or of giving offense, if the other party can under-
stand English at all (as in nine cases out of ten such a party can)
I burden him with the job of using the foreign tongue. Intil the
day comes that I can express myself correctly and confidently in
Japanese, I think I shall keep it up.
Sincerely,
Tom iSiakemne

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
October 30, 1940
Tokyo's despatch no. 4917, August 16,
1940, "Japan's New Genro".
According to the despatch, Japan has
recently witnessed the substitution of a new
advisory body, the "Jushin", replacing the old
Genro, which body of statesmen recognized by the
Emperor, formerly chose the head of each new
government.
The despatch states that since the assassi-
nations of May 15, 1932, Prince Saionji (sole
surviving member of the Genro) has participated
in the selection of the new Prime Minister only
after consultation with the "Jushin" ("most im-
portant retainers, which is composed of the
Lord Privy Seal, the President of the Privy
Council and all the former Prime Ministers.
Since 1937 Saionji has taken only a secondary
position in relation to this group and since the
war with China, the War and Navy Ministries have
played an important part on the "Jushin".
The despatch further states that the party
system of government as it existed before i932
was not deep-rooted in political Japan; the
present "Jushin" corresponds more closely with
the inherent Japanese respect for paternal ad-
visers than does the previous system appointing
as Prime

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
as Prime Minister the head of the largest
political party; the views of the "Jushin" are
very different from those prevailing during the
liberal and democratic period of Prince Saionji'
preeminence as the Emperor's adviser.
894.00/961
FE:Fales:FRE/MHP

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to report on an innovation of
fundamental importance which has recently taken place
in the political machinery whereby the prime minis-
ters of Japan are chosen. The innovation, in short,
consists of the substitution of a new advisory body
in place of the old institution known as the Genro.
This

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
121359
-2
This report is partly in the nature of a digest
of an able article entitled "Japan's New Genro" by Dr.
C. H. Spinks which appeared in the July issue of Con-
temporary Japan.
Prince Saionji is the only surviving member of a
group of imperial counselors which appeared shortly after
the Japanese Constitution was promulgated in l889. This
group, known as the Genro or elder statesmen, was com-
posed of leaders of the post-restoration period who had
been recognized by the Emperor as having rendered excep-
tional services to the state. Without constitutional
status, the Genro formed a body which was called upon by
the Emperor to choose the head of each new government.
Through the exercise of this function, the Genro was
able to exert a strong influence over the policies and
affairs of each ministry.
In the early years of constitutional government the
influence of the Genro was strongly conservative, but
with the death of the military and more reactionary
members of the group the liberal influence, typified by
Prince Saionji, became paramount. Prince Saionji was
a firm believer in party government, and during the decade
before l932 it was his practice to choose as premier the
president of the majority party or, in case of the latter's
resignation, the president of the largest minority party
in the Diet. Until the revolutionary events of 1932 it
was generally believed that this system had become so
well entrenohed that after Prince Saionji's death the
machinery would funotion entirely upon a party basis with-
out the intervention of extra-constitutional advisers.
Four

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.99)
121360
-3
Four factors, partially interrelated, may be regarded
as having prevented the system sponsored by Prince Saionji
from taking permanent root. The first was the ineptitude
and corruptness of party politics; the second was the
economic depression which settled upon Japan after the
bank crisis of l927; the third was the outbreak of a series
of political assassinations culminating in the affair of
May 15, 1932; and the fourth was the "Manchurian incident"
which may be regarded as an expression of military dis-
satisfaction with the foreign and domestic policies of
the civil administration. These factors led to the down-
fall of liberalism in Japan and ushered in the present
era of reaction.
The assassination of Premier Inukai in the May 15th
affair may be regarded as a turning point in the Japanese
system of government. Since that occasion it has been
necessary in the selection of prime ministers to give con-
sideration to the new forces of reaction against parliamentary
institutions and party government, and it has been Prince
Saionji's increasingly difficult task to select men who
could placete the reactionary elements but who at the same
time could hold those elements in check. The result has
been a complete abandonment of the system of party govern-
ment. With the appointment of Admiral Saito in 1932 until
the present, the prime ministers have without exception
been non-party bureaucrats, for the most part military or
naval men.
With the change in the type of governments has come
an equally significant change in the procedure whereby
the prime ministers are chosen. The abandonment of the
system

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.99)
121361
-4-
system of party governments was, of course, a blow to
the prestige as well as the hopes of Prince Saionji, who
was the principal sponsor of that system. Since the
assassination of Premier Inukai in the May 15th affair,
Prince Saionji has not attempted to choose a new prime
minister without consultation with other leaders. On
the occasion of the selection of Admiral Saito in 1932,
Prince Saionji came to Tokyo from his home in Okitsu to
hold conferences with all the former prime ministers,
the president of the Privy Council, the Lord Privy Seal,
and the War and Navy ministers, before reporting to the
Throne. When the Saito cabinet fell in 1934, conferences
were again held with the retiring premier, the Lord Privy
Seal, the president of the Privy Council, and the former
prime ministers. Significantly enough, in this case no
conferences were held with the War and Navy ministers.
It is generally believed that Prince Saionji sought to
establish a precedent by these meetings, aiming at the
creation of an official body which might take over the
Genro's important function of choosing premiers.
The next step in the changing process of selection of
prime ministers took place in l937 after the outbreak of
the China incident. The Hiranuma, Abe, and Yonai cabinets
all came into being after political conferences in Tokyo,
while Prince Saionji remained at his Okitsu villa and was
kept informed of the proceedings by his private secretary.
In this manner, partly because of Prince Saionji's advancing
age and feebleness, and partly because of political ten-
sion whioh has continually accompanied the bureauoratic
cabinet regime since l932 and especially since the war
with China, there has developed what virtually amounts to
a

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.99)
121362
-5-
a new Genro, or group of advisers, for the selection of
prime ministers. This new body is gradually taking over
the functions which, until l932, had been regarded as
Saionji's exclusive prerogative.
From observation of the men who have shared in the
responsibility for the formation of new cabinets since
1932, it is possible to determine with some accuracy the
composition of this new Genro to which the name "Jushin"
or "most important retainers" is becoming commonly applied.
The Lord Privy Seal, at present Marquis Koichi Kido, com-
mands foremost attention. He is an officer of ministerial
rank appointed by the Emperor, and as the first man to be
consulted by the Emperor in every cabinet change may be
regarded as key man in this new group of advisers. After
him come the president of the Privy Council, now Dr.
Yoshimichi Hara, and all former prime ministers. Those
living at present are Baron Wakatsuki, Admiral Okada,
voke
Mr. Hirota, General Hayashi, Prince Konoye, Baron Hira-
numa, General Abe, and Admiral Yonai. These men form the
body which on recent occasions has been called together
by the Lord Privy Seal to deliberate on the choice of a
new premier, but in view of the important position in Japan
of the Army and Navy, high officers of these services,
particularly the War and Navy ministers of the outgoing
cabinet, play an important role in the new system of Genro.
Although the military services are not directly represented
on the "Jushin" council, their opinion and consent has been
obtained in every instance since the outbreak of military
violence of February 26, 1936, which made plain the im-
portance of military cooperation with the civil government.
The

=== Page 331 === (confidence 1.00)
121363
-6-
The gradual evolution of this new type of Genro
may have far-reaching results on future Japanese political
development. Unlike the old Genro, which is destined to
vanish with Saionji's death and which has even now lost
its principal function, this new body, by virtue of its
peculiar membership, is permanent. It had been generally
assumed that with the passing of Prince Saionji the Genro
as an institution would also pass. This might have been
the case if the party cabinet system had not been over-
thrown in the stress of events following the incident of
May 15, 1932, and if these events had not brought about
a revulsion against liberal institutions.
It should be remembered that the party cabinet system,
as it had developed and as it existed prior to 1932, was
a distinctly new phenomenon in government and one which
had not yet struck roots deep into the political soil of
Japan. Through their long history the Japanese as a rule
have displayed an Oriental partiality for paternal advisers.
They are found not only in the political but also in the
business world as well, and even in family affairs the
ostensible head of a household is frequently dapendent upon
an "inkyo", the former head, who has withdrawn into re-
tirement. Such Genro-like advisers in government are
historically compatible with Japanese political reasoning,
and the Japanese mind, steeped for centuries in the ad-
viser tradition, is decidedly reluctant to accept any
political system in which such important decisions as the
choosing of the head of a cabinet are left to such un-
controllable forces as political parties or publio opinion.
Regardless of the extent to which the May 15 incident
changed the course of political development in Japan, the
party

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.98)
121364
-7-
party cabinet system was, after all, an alien institution
which had never been fully acclimated to the peculiar
Japanese environment. On the other hand, the original
Genro, of which Prince Saionji is the last representative,
and the new Genro or "Jushin" council, which is now crys-
talizing as an institution, are infinitely truer to Japa-
nese tradition.
It may be added that the appearance of this new Genro
in an era of bureaucratic cabinets is a vivid reflection
of the vast changes which have come over Japan's political
complexion. When party cabinets were in vogue, public
opinion, working through the parties and the Diet, played
a small but growing part in the choice of premiers and in
the determination of policies. Today this manifestation
of public opinion, which was generally on the side of
liberalism, has been largely submerged while the reaction-
ary elements in the nation have been accorded a stronger
position. Not the least reason for this change is the fact
that the new institution assuming responsibility for the
selection of prime ministers is composed to a considerable
extent of men who hold views in sympathy with the reaction-
ary, anti-liberal forces in the country. While the new
Genro is by no means the tool of the reactionaries and
although it contains elements which may at least be termed
independent, yet its views are very different from those
prevailing during the liberal and democratic decade of
Prince Sainnji's preeminence as the Emperor's adviser.

SonebhG. Jree

Tokyo's despatch no. 4946, September 5,
1940, "The 'New National Structure' in
Japan".
The despatch states that the war has
revealed the need for a change in parlia-
mentary government; that party politics
and politicians were corrupt and inefficient,
and, fostered by the army, a popular revul-
sion arose against them; that an effort to
coordinate and integrate all aspects of
national life began with a planned economy,
which led to political renovation. The
despatch further states that during the
winter Diet of 1940 various political
parties decided to disband, possibly moti-
vated by frustration of their ambitions
and desire to get aboard the new band wagon;
Prince Konoye, president of the Privy
Council, resigned in order to assume
leadership of the new movement and has
tried to discourage the idea of a mere
re-shuffling of political forces; the
last party to disband was the Minseito, which
advocated the formation of a "new political
structure".
According to Konoye, this new structure
aims at the concentration and unification
of

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.99)

JIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2

subordination and readjustment of the
netional life to the attainment of objec-
tives in China; it envisages no alteration
of the constitution; its motive is unity
of the supreme command, the legislature,
the administration and the people for the
achievement of their goal; its nucleus
will be the Cabinet but its driving force
should be political power based on the
organized people; it transcends the idea
of a mere single political party; it may
have to go through many stages before it
attains perfection; it is to be permanent;
it will embrace not only politics but
economy, education, culture, and all
domains of the life of the State in which
the Japanese people are to fulfill their
duty of assisting the Throne.
The despatch states that, with the
decline of the political parties, the
army has usurped their place as the driv-
ing force behind Japanese politics.
Konoye says: "The need of the moment 1s
to arrange for the military and civilian
population to cooperate from the depth of
their hearts." According to the despatch,

Sir:
I have the honor to report on the movement for a
"new national structure" in Japan.
Over a period of several years the Embassy has
reported the development of a movement in Japan directed
toward the formation of a single party or organization
in which the political power of the nation would be unified.
(Vide

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.99)
121367
-2-
(Vide despatches nos. 2268, February 18, 1937, 2722,
January 6, 1938, 3380, November 1, 1938, 3505, Decem-
ber 3, 1938). There is little to de ained by reviewing
in detail the maneuverings of the leaders of this move-
ment over a period of years, but it is believed that a
short description of the history and background of the
movement is essential to the com rehension of a political
development that is perplexingly vague as well as highly
significant.
The history of tho new structure movement is bound
up with Japanese military actions on the continent during
the past decade. With the progress of hostilities every
form of national effort has been increasingly subordinated
to the attainment of military and political objectives in
China. The inevitable consequence of this mustering of
Japan's material and spiritual resources has been a change
in virtually every aspect of Japanese life. Above all,
there has been a critical reexamination of the machinery
and institutions of government adopted perhaps too hastily
in the early years of contact with the West. Although it
is not accurate to say that the decline of parliamentary
government can be attributed entirely to the conflict with
China, yet it is true that the stresses arising out of that
Fumaro
A conflict have revealed the need for change. Prince Konoye
OBF
voiced the feelings of Japanese in a recent declaration to
the effect that the military situation in China and the
developments in Europe made it essential for Japan to effeot
a reorganization of its political structure.
Since

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.99)
121368
-3-
Since the adoption of a parliamentary form of govern-
ment under the constitution of 1889, the system has been
a facade behind which rirst one powerful influence and then
another hus ruled the country. Until the early nineteen
twenties the so-called "Genro" dominated, but with the passing
of most of these personages a truly parliamentary régime was
beginning to evolve u. d r the sponsorship of Prince Saionji,
the last and most liberal of the "Genro".
The political
parties which developed during this period thrived so long
as mon with capacity for leadership could be found to head
them, but in later yeurs the parties were led by hacks with
little ability and less vision. The parties, beset with
factional jealousies and strife, degenerated into self-
seeking gangs whose doings were notorious for corruption.
The immorality of the politicians of this period stood in
striking contrast with the high standarās of loyalty and
patriotism prevailing among the bureaucracy, especially the
army and navy, and created grave distrust of party politicians
and party government in the mind of the public. In times of
prosperity such as prevailed in the early and middle twenties
the abuses of party politics were regarded with some toler-
ance by the nation, but with the coming of economic depression
in the late twenties and in the stress of the effort caused
by the hostilities in China, the attitude of the people began
to change.
In the months following the outbreak of the Manchuria
incident of September 18, 1931, which aroused a wave of
intense patriotic ardor, there began a series of terroristio
activities

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.99)
121369
-4-
activities by reactionary-military groups directed at
the politicians and against parliamentary overnment.
Prime ministers, cabinet ministers, politicians and
financiers fell uider the knives or bullets of the
demonstrators. Such incidents as those of May 15, 1932,
and February 36, 1936, underscored with srim emphasis
the growing discontent. All such demonstrations which
have given such an exciting tone to the Japanese political
scene in recent years, have been interpreted by the
people as protests against the existing political order
and as demands for a political system more in keeping with
the national spirit.
The February 26, 1936, incident is frequently pointed
to as the beginning of the present movement for a "new
structure", for it was in the shock of that ominous demon-
stration by the Emperor's own troops that it first was
realized that some far-reaching change in the system of
government was necessary if Japan was to be saved from
revolution. From that time strong reactionary elements
began to work together toward the abolition of parliamentary
government and the substitution therefor of one powerful
nationalist body. It is not necessary to recount in detail
the movements and maneuvers by the various groups endeavor-
ing to alter the scheme of government; those of any signifi-
cance have been dealt with in contemporary despatches.
At

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.99)
121370
-5-
At this point it is necessary to mention a movement
generally referred to as "national mobilization", of which
the political movement towurd a "new structure" may be
regarded as a part. Hational mobilization is described as the
effort to coordinate and integrate all aspects of national
activity for the carrying out of Japan's military objectives.
A planned economy is one aspect, which has already been
brought into general effect by a series of laws and regula-
tions dating back to the beginning of the China conflict.
The results of these measures indicate clearly that radical
and permanent changes in the economic and industrial system
are rapidly taking place. While these changes in the
economic field were takins place it was natural that at
the same time thougnt should turn to an overhaul of a system
of government that had proven itself to be unsuitable to
conditions in Japan. National mobilization has thus in-
evitably led to political renovation.
Whatever the eventual fate of the "new structure"
movement, Prince Konoye's name will always be connected
with it. In his first cabinet, which lasted from June 1937,
to January 1939, comprising the first stage of the China
incident, Prince Konoye was persuaded by various reformists
of the need of a national party to assist the government
in solving the problems growing out of the hostilities.
Urged by these reformists, he appointed a commission of
three, one of whom was the reactionary Admiral Suyetsugu,
to study and report upon the feasibility of organizing a
new

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.98)
121371
-6-
7
new party. Unhappily the plan evclved failed to meet with
the Prince's view of what the new political system should be,
and the matter was temporarily shelved. Incidentally,
Suyetsugu was reported to be piqued by the rejection of the
proposed plan, and since then has not ceased to interfere
with Prince Konoye's further efforts to organize a new party.
Except for sporadic efforts by individuals, interest in
the new party movement subsided until the Diet session in
the winter of 1940, at a moment when public confidence in
party politics had descended to a new low on account of the
disgraceful antics of the politicians in connection with the
Saito affair. A contemporary editorial in the ASAHI SHIMBUN
voiced public feeling in the following words: "We feel
more strongly than ever that the Imperial Diet is confront-
ed by the urgent necessity of introducing reforms; present
political exigencies and general signs point to the in-
evitability of some vital change". Major-General Muto,
KiD
Director of the Military Affairs Bureau made a profound
OBF
impression on the nation when he declared in the Diet:
"If the political parties are engrossed in their own in-
terests and maneuvers, they had better be dissolved."
In the wave of public disgust following the events
of the Saito affair, a number of members of the Lower House
who had seen the handwriting on the wall formed a "League
for the Fulfillment of the Objectives of the Sacred War"
which quickly found adherents in and out of the Diet.
It

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.96)
121372
-7-
It sought to fan into active flame the reformist new
party movement which had long been smouldering through-
out the Diet. Other organizations and groups of similar
character took fom, numberin; amons their nembers Ryutaro
Nagai, long a stalwart of the Minseito, Finance Minister
Sakurauchi, and Count Arima. Associated with them direct-
ly or indirectly were Adniral Duyetsugu, General Hayashi,
Kulo
Baron Hiranuma, Fusanosuke Kuhara and others. The move-
ment needed only a leader. Baron Hiranuma and Prince
3OBF'S
Konoye were regarded as best qualified.
Events took a decisive turn when on IMay 4 Fusanosuke
Kuhara, the not-too reputable president of the Kuhara
Faction of the Seiyukai, proposed publicly that all
existing parties be cissolved as a preliminary move
toward the formation of a single new party. The pro-
posal at first met with opposition, perhaps because of
general distrust of Kuhara's motives. Later, under the
weight of circumstances that will be described, the
political parties decided to disband. The last to do
so was the powerful Minseito, which ended a 60-year career
on August 15, having adopted a resolution advocating the
formation of a "new political structure". No doubt many
((u)
of the party members, faced by an impasse to their am-
(ald dised)
bitions, plumped for dissolution in the hope of re-
assembling on the new band-wagon of a national party.
Prince Konoye has repeatedly tried to discourage the
idea of a mere reshuffling of political forces but it
still persists.
Prinoe

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.97)
121373
-8-
Prince Konoye, who had been appointed President of
the Privy Council after the fall of his first government,
was generally regarded as the one man capable of success-
fully creating a "new structure". Aside from the fact
that his name was linked with the movement curing his
first premiership, the prestige of his ancient lineage
renders him singularly qualified to lead a movement for
the unification of the nation. What pressure was used to
persuade him to assume leadership of the movement has not
been revealed, but he was undoubtedly actuated by the
highest sentiments of duty and loyalty. On June 24 he
894.00
resigned from the Privy Council, stating in his letter of
resignation that he vished to be free of his official

6
political structure which he believed to be of urgent
3
importance. He later explained his position in a verbal

in whole as shedding light on his objectives (Domei version):
"The necessity of firmly establishing a
powerful national political structure, to meetthe
unprecedentedly disturbing internal and external
situation conironting the country today, is recog-

which has become increasingly active, will be an

toward the attainment of this objective. However,
I would not be able to participate in this movement
if it merely were designed to effect a reshuffle
