Unti1

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
Until this announcement by Prince Konoye, the move-
ment for political renovation seemed to envisage merely
a fusion of the existing parties or a new party to be
built on the basis of those parties. The talk was of a
new political party rather than of a new political struc-
ture. Even at the present time on the part of the gen-
eral public an extraordinary diversity of opinion exists
in regard to the form and direction of the new movement.
Some conceive of the "new structure" in terms of European
fascism; others seem to view it as something akin to the
"New Life" movement in China which places strong emphasis
on morals and conduct; while still others regard it as
simply a coalition party movement. My despatoh no. 4928
of August 30, 1940, describes the confusion in the public
mind concerning a movement which seems to be all things to
all men. But in his statement of August 28 Prince Konoye
made it plain that the new structure as he conceived it
transcends the idea of a mere single political party.
"The movement is highly political in nature", he said,
"but it is by no means a movement for a political party....
It aims at the concentration and unification of the nation's
entire powers. Its activities extend to the whole life of
the nation".
What, then, is the "new national structure"? The
only reliable source from which a reply to this question
may be sought are the various utterances of Prince Konoye,
especially his address of August 28 before the Preparatory
Committee for the New National Struoture, a translation of
1/
which is appended. But even these announcements are more
in

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
in the nature of declarations of objectives and principles
than the definition of a concrete program. The fact is
that at the present time probauly meither Prince Konoye
nor anyone else has a clear idea of the form in which the
"new structure" will emerge. The task of appraising the
"new structure" itself must therefore be reserved for a
later report. However, a few general comments based on
Prince Konoye's statements, may not be out of place.
In his statement at Karuizawa on July 7 Prince Konoye
made it plain that he contemplated no alteration of the
Constitution of Japan and that therefore the New Structure
must be fitted into the franework of that instrument.
Nor is the Diet or even the existing political parties
to be ignored, he declared. The following quotations are
from the Domei version of his remarks: "The motive of the
new political structure is unity of the supreme command,
the legislature, the administration, and the people at
large so they can be merged into one for the achievement
of their goal....The Cabinet may take a different form
and relations between the Cabinet and the people will be
considered in a new light.......The nucleus of the new struc-
ture naturally will be the Cabinet but the axis and driv-
ing force of the new structure should be political power
based on the organized people.....The new political
structure perhaps will have to go through many stages
before it attains perfection." In his statement on
August 28 he declared that the national structure is to be
2
a

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.99)
12137.4
-11-
a permanent and not a temporary movement to tide over
the emergency period. It will embrace not only politics
but economy, education, culture, and "all the domains of
the life of the State and of the people." "In short", he
declared, "the new national structure means a nation-wide
and permanent organization in which the Japanese people in
all walks of life are to fulfill their duty of assisting
the Throne". Fine words, whatever they may mean when trans-
lated into work-a-day politics!
Obviously the crux of the "new structure" movement
hinges on the relationship between the military and the
civil administrations. Japan's political disputes of
recent years have largely centered around the question
of the army's political power. With the decline of the
parties, the army has usurped their place as the driging
force behind Japanese politics, and in spite of consti-
tutional impediments has openly exercised political power
in all directions. What has obviously been taking place
in Japan during the past decade is a reversion to old forms
and traditions fixed during centuries of rule by a military
caste. Now, of course, the army is no longer a caste,
having democratized itself by conscription from the masses,
but there remains unchanged in the nation's consciousness
the military tradition and, even more important, the tradi-
tion of military title to political power. The principal
problem before Prince Konoye is, therefore, to find a
means to associate the Supreme Military Command with the
Cabinet in the general administration. His statements
show

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.99)
121375
-12-
show that he is thoroughly aware of this problem. In a
press interview on June 4 he declared (Domei):
"A new
party which is ready to do the bidding of the military is
not what I am after. A new party desired by me is one
confident enough to tender advice to the military and
above all to reflect the feelings of the people in what
is done by tho military.
The military should have the
responsibility of managing military affairs. It is up to
the politicians to manage political affairs.
There is no
thought of putting pressure on the army or resisting it.
The need of the moment is to arrange for the military
and the civilian population to cooperato fron the depth
of their hearts". Again, in his formal statement of
July 28 he declared "Among the items to be considered in
this new organization of the nation must be mentioned the
harmonious cooperation between the High Command and the
administrative branch of the Government....."
In its attitude toward the "new structure" movement
the Army seems to be wavering between desire to be rid
of the incumbrance of parliamentary government and re-
luctance to see set up any authority capable of challenging
its own power. The military attitude toward the plans now
under consideration are attracting the closest attention
because it is generally realized that the fate of the
"new structure", and in a broader sense the fate of con-
stitutional government in Japan, depends on the stand taken
by the military at this time. Judging from the latest
press

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.99)
121376
-13-
press reports, the opinion now prevailin~ in the army is
that in order to preserve its special status as an organ
of the Supreme Command it should "stand outside of the
new structure and offer its wholehearted cooperation from
outside". This would mean, of course, that the old con-
flict of authority would be perpetuated in the "new
structure".
The composition of the commission appointed by
Prince Konoye on August 25 to organize the new structure
is significant. Of the 26 members, the following will be
recognized as representing the blackest reaction: Colonel
Kingoro Hashimoto, president of the Dai Nippon Young Men's
Party, who was implicated in the sinking of the U.S.S. PANAY,
Seigo Nakano, president of the defunct and ultra-nationalistic
Tohokai, Toshihisa Kuzuu, head of the notorious Amur (Black
Dragon) Society, Toshio Shiratori, the nationalistic ex-
Ambassador to Italy, Admiral Nobumasa Suyetsugu, a notor-
ious fire-eater. Representing more moderate elements are
Yoshiaki Hatta, President of the Japan Chamber of Commerce
and Industry, Dr. Hiraga, President of the Tokyo Imperial
University, Mr. Takashi Isaka, Director of the Industrial
Club, etc. But by and large the commission has a strong
flavor of the most virulent nationalism. It is not hard
to predict the nature of any project emerging from t his Cave
of Abdullum.
Many

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
121377
-14-
Many observers have spoken of the "new structure"
movement as an effort to create a Japanese equivalent
of totalitarian régi es in Europe. However, Japanese ex-
ponents of the Nev Structure take much trouble to refute
this impression. Prince Konoye himself has said in his
statement of August 28 "No matter what brilliant results
such a system (totalitarianism) may have reaped in other
lands, it is not acceptable in Japan because it is con-
trary to the basic principle of our national policy....."
On the sane occasion he declared that the rivilege of
assisting the Throne is cormon to all Japanese subjects and
"cannot be monopolized by the power of a single individual
or a single party." Actually, the contention that the
"new structure" will differ essentially from fascist
organizations in Europe appears to be sound. Dictator-
ship by an individual is not in confo mity with Japanese
political traditions and could probably never be implanted
in Japan.
Despite these arguments, the fact remains that the
impelling force behind the "new structure" movement in
Japan is the same as that which brought into being Fascism
and Naziism, namely the necessity of unifying the total
energies of the people to meet the exigencies of the occasion.
In Prince Konoye's own words his movement is designed "to
unite the total energies of the State and of the People"
in order to make possible "the powerful pursuance of any
 policy when the necessity arises". Again he declared:
"It

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.99)
121378
-15-
"It aims at the concentration and unification of the
nation's entire powers. Its activities extend to the whole
life of the nation". In so far as fascism is characterized
by authoritative government; by a monopoly political organi-
zation; by subordination of tho individual to the larger
interest of the state; and by rigid control over the economic
life of the nation, Japan is following closely the pattern
set by the dictatorships.
The trend toward national social-
ism is already well under way. Industry, finance, politics
and national defense are becoming inextricably interwoven
in the totalitarian pattern.
It is therefore fruitless to argue whe her the "new
structure" vill follow the European pattern of totalitarian-
ism and whether the plan for a "new structure" would have
been developed without European examples. It is safe to
say that given the examples of successful authoritarianism
in Europe and given an emergency situation at home, the
Japanese are busy exercising at once their genius for
imitation and their sure sense of tradition. While it
is yet impossible to appraise with any degree of accuracy
a political structure that is still on the drafting
board of its designers, yet by the utterances of its
sponsors the "structure" has every earmark of a totalitarian
régime, in its purposes not far different from the Nazi
party system in Germany or the Fascist party system in
Italy, but in form modified to suit Japanese traditions
and ideas. Two years ago, in my despatch no. 3380 of
November 1, 1938, the following estimate was made of
the

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.96)
121379
-16-
the national mobilization movement in Japan, an estimate
which, I believe, stands rood vith respect also to the
"new structure" movement:
"As drastic as are the changes to be carried out
under national mobilization, we do not believe that there
will emerge a state which in its su erficial aspects and in
many of its funetional aspects, will be on all fours with
Germany and Italy. It is unlikely that the national polity
will be altered or that there will be material revision of
894.00
the organic laws of the several branches of the Government.
Nor do we think it likely that a personal dictatorship
would be tolerated. We envisage rather a retention and
strengthening of the Emperor cult, and unification of the
9
various elements which now rule the country, perhaps even-
3
tually taking form in an organization by which the masses
and all forms of national activity will be regimented,
coordinated and integrated. What is evolving is totali-
tarianism, if one insists on usi s that term, but it will
be totalitarianism sui generis".
Respectfully yours,
pmon. F

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. / to despatch
no. ya46 dated Sept. 5, 1440
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Address by the Prime Minister,
Prince Fumimaro onoye, at the
first session of the Preparatory
Committee for the Mew National
Structure, August 28, 1940.
In the midst of a vorlawide disturbance, Japan is
now going forward with an unparalleled task, the creation
of a new order in East Asia. If Japan is to bring the
China Affair to a successful conclusion, while adjusting
herself to the international situation, and to take an
active part in the establishment of a nev world order,
she must concentrate on t is task the total power of the
nation to the utmost degree so as to be in a position to
take in an independent manner, swiftly and resolutely,
appropriate measures for meeting whatever situation may
arise. To this end Japan must perfect a national defense
structure of the highest degree. The basis of such a
structure is a powerful internal structure. Consequently
there has arisen the pressing demand for the establishment
of a new structure in politics, economy, education, culture
and all the domains of the life of the State and of the people.
This indeed is a national demand, transcending a Cabinet,
a faction or an individual. It is not a demand of a temporary
character for carrying out any specific policy, but of a
permanent one, for rendering possible the powerful pursuance
of any policy, when the necessity arises. Whether or not
Japan can establish such a strong national structure will
decide

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
decide the very rise or fall of the nation.
Among the items to be considered in this new organi-
zation of the nation must be mentioned the harmonious co-
operation between the High Command and the administrative
branch of the Government, the consolidation of St te mecha-
nisms and the heightening of efficiency, and the establish-
ment of a new parliamentary structure as ar organ for assist-
ing the Throne.
. The Government, on their oart, are exerting
utmost efforts in order to achieve these ends. But of far
greater importance is the firm establishment of that 'national
structure' which is to serve as the very foundation of all
and under which the people are to fulfil effectively their
duty of assisting the Throne. It is with this end in view
that the present Preparatory Comittee has been convened
and that I request your deliberations and collaboration.
The aim of the new national structure is to unite the
total energies of the State and of the peo le, to make one
living whole of our hundred million fellow countrymen and
enable them all to fulfil perfectly their duty as subjects
of the Throne. To attain this goal, each one of us must
be enabled to fulfil that duty in the performance of his
daily task. It is but natural that when the majority of
the people, as it has been the case in the past, have no
opportunity to take part in government other than when they
are called to cast a vote once every three or four years,
they would not as a whole take to heart the destiny of the
country.
The organization of the nation is that which enables
the people to serve the nation in their everyday life: it
must

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
must therefore extend to the economic and cultural spheres.
There must be a solid nation-wide structure in which all
component parts (whether economic or cultural) are organized
vertic lly, and they ere also bound together horizontally.
It is because ther: does not exist such a structure allowing
the people to assist effectively the Throne that we see today
a tendency towards a conflict,(in economic and cultural fields,)
between those who govern and those who are governed, a lack
of true understanding on the part of the authorities who
formulate the policies of the people's real activities and
an indifference on the part of the people towards the formu-
lation of State policies.
When we look at things in this manner, the fundanental
points of the national structure appear clearly: the people
should be enabled to take part, from the inside, in the es-
tablishing of the country's economic and cultural policies
and at the same time these policies should reach all the
peripheries of national life. It is only under these con-
ditions that the will and ideas of both those who govern
and those who are governed can be fully appreciated by each
other and that the total power of the nation can be concen-
trated on carrying out the policies of the nation.
A definite national movement is necessary for the suc-
cesful realization of this national structure. Such a move-
ment should spontaneously spring from the people themselves.
If it is planned or guided by the Government, or it is given
an administrative structure, it may hinder the spontaneous
manifestation of the people's energies. The present circum-
stances. nowever, do not allow us to rely only on the spon-
taneous development of such a movement. Moreover, agitations
from

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
from below, are liable to degenerate into factional strifes
and fail to expand into a really national movement. The
Government have thus found it necessary to take positive
steps for fostering and directing this movement.
Viewed in this light, this movement (for a national
structure) is a common (national) underteking of both the
Government and the people: it is a nation-wide movement
to assist the Throne. It is not merely a spiritual movement
in the narrow sense but aims at enhancing the political
ideals and the political consciousness of the nation. And
the choosing of men of talent, known or unknown, from all
strata of society to form the nucleus of the movement and
thus to obtain a strong political power and driving force
is the first and indispensable step that should be taken.
The movement (thus) is highly political in nature, but
it is by no means a movement for a political party. Indi-
vidual and sectional interests ana attitudes are necessarily
in the very nature of a political party. It is true that
there can be no whole without parts; and to condemn parties
only because they comprise separate elements is not necessarily
fair. It may be said that in those times when liberalism
was the basis of economic activities, the existence of politi-
cal parties was justified. It must be acinowledged that in
Japan herself the partis stood up against the (bureaucratic)
influences to make heard the voice of the people. But never-
theless it cannot be denied that the past activities of the
parties often were not in keeping with the essential mission
of the Diet, which is to assist the Throne.
The new national structure movement aims at superseding
the old party politics postulated upon liberalism. It is
essentially national, all embracing and public-spirited in
character

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
aracter. It aims at the concentration and unification
of the nation's entire powers. Its activities extend to
the whole life of the nation. Even were this movement to
rise as a ropular movement, its e ruster would not he that
of a political party in the old sense. It would on the
contrary be a national movenent unding above any political
party, embracing all parties and factions, economic and
cultural bodies, ano unitin l i the spirit of public
service. hen such a movement is led by the Government
itself, it cannot, in any sense, be a party movement. Those
who hold the reins of Government and are entrusted with the
task of assisting the Throne, are always placed in a position,
where they must seek the vwelfare of the whole but never be
permitted to indulge in party politics which, in their very
nature, contain elements of sectional antagonism and conflict.
As I have just stated, the national structure cannot
take the form of a political party, especially when it is led
by the Government. Neither can it be allowed to take the
form of a single party system. This political system takes
a "part" and makes of it a "whole"; consider the State and
the Party as a one and the same thing, it views any opposition
to the Party as a revolt against the State; it renders per-
manent the ruling position of one Party with the head of that
Party as a permanent wielder of the power to govern. No matter
what brilliant results such a system may have reaped in other
lands, it is not acceptable in Japan because it is contrary
to the basic principle of our national polity of "One Sovereign
over all". In Japan, it is the privilege of all His Imperial
Majesty's

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
Majesty's subjects to assist the Throne and that privilege
cannot be monopolized by the power of either a single indi-
vidual or a single party.
If there sho ld arise a diffe ence of opinion con-
cerning the assistance to be offered, the final decision
would rest with the Throne. And once an Imperial decision
has been given, all the subjects of the Throne should unite
in obeying His ajesty's Word. Thut is (must be) the true
essence of Japanese polity.
In short, the new national structure means a nation-
wide and permanent organization in which the Japanese
people in all walks of life are to fulfil their duty of
assisting the Throne.
Although the perfection of t is structure is by no
means an easy task, the Gcvernment are convinced that it
provides the best eans for surmounting the difficulties
of these times.
His Imperial Majesty the Emperor was pleased to grant
a message on February 1l of this year, showing to His sub-
jects the way in which we should face the present situation.
The Government, in obedience to the Imperial Word, are taking
the lead in this national movement to assist the Throne.
They are resolved to overcome the great obstacles that con-
front our country, and to fulfil the heavy responsibilities
which are theirs to 'guard and maintain the prosperity of
the Imperial Throne.'
I have invited eminent authorities from Government,
Army and Navy circles and also from outside the Government
to join this Preparatory Committee for the National structure,
and

=== Page 365 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
nd I ask that you will study, and collaborate in solving,
the questions relative to the general lines of the organi-
zation, the creation of a central organ for the national
movement, and the adjustment of its relations with existing
organizations as well as its co-ordination with the structure
of the State.
Note: Portions included in parentheses appear in
the Japanese original but are omitted in the
official translation.

Tokyo's despatch no. 5037, October 7, 1940,
The 'New Structure' in the Headlines".
The despatch emphasizes the extraordinary,
sweeping and rapid changes now taking place in
the life and thought of the Japanese people,
and states that old values are being discarded
and that every phase of life is now regimented.
It is mentioned that the courteous, reasonable
officials of the Foreign Office have been
superseded by the carpet-bagger type seen in
China.
In order to give a picture of the situa-
tion, the despatch quotes from the Tokyo
English language press a number of headlines
and topical sentences. Among these are:
"All Foreign Newsreels to be Processed
Here in Harmony with Times."
"National Policy Garb 1s Considered
Correct for Formal Functions."
"Salesmen Will Not be Fermitted to Solicit
Customers for Unnecessary Goods . . . the New
Mode of Simple Life."
"Good-will and Cultural Societies, Includ-
ing the America-Japan Society, Are to Come
Under . . . a Central Organization."
"Uniform

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.99)
EPARTMENT OF STATE
VISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
"Uniform Wearing May be Enforced .
Commission to be Picked to Design Standard Garb
for Women."
"Rotary Club Federation Decides on
Dissolution."
"Single Labor Body Being Established ..

I have the honor to refer to my despatch No.'4928
of August 30, 1940, in regard to the "new structure" in
Japan.
In that despatch were quoted the headlines of articles
chosen at random from the press, to illustrate the extra-
ordinary changes now taking place in the life and thought
of
1

=== Page 371 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
of the Japanese people. These changes are so sweeping and
so rapid that the old foreign resident of this country
must rub his eyes to recognize the Japan ti.at is now
emerging. Old ideas, old habits, old values and old in-
terests are fast being tossed into the discard. The face
of the new Japan is hard, suspicious and grim; every
phase of life is regimented; the watchword of the day
is "strength without joy".
The Japanese friends of a few months ago who
represent the graceful and amiable side of Japanese life
have dropped away. They dare not be seen in the company
of foreigners for fear of questioning by the police.
Wherever the foreigner goes at present he is an object
of suspicion at whom small boys shout "spy!". At the
Foreign Office the courteous, reasonable gentlemen with
whom one formerly dealt have disappeared into retirement;
their places have been taken by men who look and act
like traffic policemen. An officer on my staff who
was formerly in China remarked recently "These ofricials
whom we now meet in Tokyo are the same type as the carpet-
baggers one sees in China".
Perhaps a review of selected articles from the press
will give a better picture of what is happening in Japan
than any attempt at description. Even a recitation of
the headlines with a brief quotation from each article is
revealing. The following appeared in the local English-
language press during September:
"Cartoonists Regimented - All cartoonists will have
to confine themselves to subjects in accordance
with the spirit of the times, and 'create a new
order in cartoons' if they wish to continue their
work."

December 9, 1940
MMH:
Tokyo's despatch no. 5lo6, November 1
1940, entitled "The New Structure in Japan"
transmits a copy of a monograph written by
Hugh Byas, Tokyo correspondent for the New
York Times and the London Times.
The Embassy states that "the enclosed
paper will be recognized as an authoritative
appraisal of an important subject".
The enclosure sets forth that:
The transformation of national life
now taking place in Japan under the name
"New Structure" movement has been primarily
motivated and made necessary by the dis-
covery that Japan, as at present organized,
1s not strong enough to complete her plans
in China.
To formulate a plan for the "New Structure"
Prince Konoye selected a heterogeneous com-
mittee which promised little hope of agreement,
but after six sessions it produced a plan
which establishes a Fascist-like organization
for the purpose of organizing a Fascist-
l1ke state. However, unlike the case of
3
by
040

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.99)
JEPARTMENT OF STATE
VISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2
by the Fascists, in Japan it is the Govern-
ment which is transforming the State into
a totalitarian cooperetive structure, and
creating a Fascist movement by official
edict. As Prince Konoye pointed out,
changes in Japan always come "from above"
and further, according to General Araki,
"a country ruled by divinely descended
emperors has no need of a dictator".
The plan agreed upon by Konoye's com-
mittee provides for twin organizations, the
"Imperial Rule Assistance Movement", end the
"Imperial Rule Assistance Association". The
Movement functions through an Executive
Council which is to "convey the will and
ideas of those who govern to those who are
governed". The Association functions
through a Central Cooperative Council which
1s to "convey the will and ideas of those
who are governed to those who govern", and
is to have Branch Cooperative Councils in
every prefecture, city and village. The
president of the Executive Council (Movement)
1s appointed by the Prime Minister and he
in turn selects the members of his Executive
Council and also nominates the members of
the Central Cooperative Council (Association),
but one-half of these must be from names
submitted by the Prefectural Cooperative
Councils.
Although

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.99)
EPARTMENT OF STATE
VISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
Although the Army has promised to co-
operate with the new structure, it has re-
fused to become a part of it and has stated
that it will maintain its independent posi-
tion of responsibility to the Emperor alone.
ThEyen though the Army has perpetuated
the old division of authority the movement
must not be considered to have failed. Im-
pressive steps have been made, the political
parties are dissolved, and under the auspices
of representatives of the iron and steel,
coal, electric power, shipoing and other
industries, a "Plan for a New Economic
System for the People", and a "Plan for an
All-Japan Industrial Federation" have been
approved, which may indicate that business
men are to be entrusted with the execution
of the economic policy. The example of the
political parties and business leaders is
being followed by various other types of
organizations and the people are rushing to
obey.
If, however, the vital economic frame-
work of the "New Structure" is being
created by business itself, and if religious
and other organizations are being unified
under Government pressure, what is there
left for the Movement" to do? The enswer is
that the "Movement" and "Association" are
the machinery by which public opinion is to
be led.

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 5106 of November 1, 1940,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Study on "The 'New National Structure' in Japan" by Mr.
Hugh Byas, Tokyo Correspondent for the New York Times
and the London Times.
On June 24 Prince Konce resigned the presidency of
the Privy Council in order that he might be free to form
a "new political structure". While he was still discus-
sing ideas with his "brain trust" the cabinet was suddenly
overthrown by the Army and Konoe was called to form a
government. One of his first actions was the appointment
of a committee of 26 to prepare plans for the new structure.
Prince Konoe opened the deliberations of the com-
mittee on August 23 with an address defining the new
structure as "a nation-wide and permanent organization in
which the Japanese people in all walks of life are to ful-
fil their duty of assisting the Throne." Japan was to
create "a national defence state of the highest degree"
based upon a powerful internal structure comprising "all
the domains of the life of the state and of the people."
This transormation was necessary (the Prince stated) to
enable Japan to bring the China affair to a successful
conclusion, to adjust herself to the new international
situation, and to take an active part in the establishment
of a new world order.
Of these three reasons the first is clearly the im-
portant one. It recognizes that while Japan's hands are
tied in China she cannot freely take advantage of the
opportunities that the European war may open, and admits
that Japan is unable to finish the China war without strength-
ening her whole national organization. Coming from the
general to the particular, Prince Konoe asked the Committee
to

=== Page 413 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
to consider means of attaining harmonious cooperation
between the High Command and the "administretive branch of
the government", i.e., the Cabinet; the consolidation of
state mechanisms and the establishment of a new parliamen-
tary structure for assisting the Throne. Those remarkably
indefinite words "assisting the Throne" are constantly
used by young officers and other "reformist" elements to
describe the role of the people in the administration of
the country. They have not defined the nature, or method
of this popular assistance --- but have used it as an al-
ternative to the parliamentary system which was accused of
having allowed political parties and capitalists to come
between the Emperor and his people.
With this general direction the preparatory committee
set to work. The committee, chosen by Prince Konoe, was
a remarkable body. Its composition was so mixed that there
seemed little hope of either unanimous agreement or ef-
ficient legislative work. But the members took an oath
that they would work sincerely and Prince Konoe gave them
practical assistance in the form of a powerful and efficient
secretariat comprising high-ranking military and naval of-
ficers and civil servants. Those officials knew what was
needed and after six sessions the committee produced a
plan.
Briefly, the plan establishes a fascist-like organi-
zation which is to organize a fascist-like State. One
striking difference between the Italian and the Japanese
systems is that in Italy the Fascists overthrew the
government, took possession of the State and transformed
it; in Japan the government is transforming the State into
a totalitarian corporative structure and creating, by
official

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
official edict, a fascist movement as part of the machinery
of change. To Western minds it is an artificial and topsy-
turvy way of proceeding. But as Prince Konoe pointed out,
changes in Japan always come "from above". Another vital
difference between Japanese and European totalitarianism
is the absence of a dictator. Clumsily but correctly the
Japanese call their country "an Emperor-centric state".
In General Araki's words "a country ruled by divinely
descended Emperors has no need of a dictator"; a re-
lationship such as that which exists between Mussolini and
the King of Italy would revolt the Japanese. The new plan
adopts the "leader principle" to the extent of having its
executive members nominated and not elected, and its head
is a president appointed by the Prime Minister.
The people find nothing incongruous in the idea of
government-made party which they shall all join in the
spirit of the conscript joining the regiment he will
afterwards call"his". In much the same way was the Con-
stitution conferred on the nation. It is characteristic
of Konoe, and the age, that whereas Prince Ito searched
Europe for examples and finally launched the Constitution
with the utmost solemnity of Imperial sanction after high
debate, Konoe tossed his ideas to a secretariat and an
ad hoc committee which produced a plan after half-a-dozen
meetings. But less formality means greater flexibility.
The makers of the new structure were handicapped by the
existence of a Constitution held sacrosanct because con-
ferred by the great Emperor Meiji. Konoe said the Con-
stitution must be respected; the new structure is therefore
being

=== Page 415 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
being erected awkwardly beside the old one. The new
structure is not guarded with extraordinary Imperial
sanctions and can be changed by the same means that created
it --- the will of the government.
The new structure of the committee's report is dual ---
a central headquarters called Taisei Yokusan Undo "Imperial
Rule Assistance Movement", and Taisei Yokusan Kai "Imperial
Rule Assistance Association". It again suggests the topsy-
turvydom of the Far East that the "Movement" is strongly
centralised bureaucratic executive body without organic
connection with the people but the committee were handicapped
in the search for a name by the fact that the word "party"
is taboo because of its odour of liberalism. The Movement
consists of a nominated Executive Council, with president,
vice-president, advisers, permanent directors and an
elaborate organization. Its twin, the Association, is a
Central Cooperative Council with branches in every prefec-
ture, city and village. Even the Cho-Kai (neighborhood
associations of householders) are to find a place. The
Executive Council will "convey the will and ideas of those
who govern to those who are governed"; the Cooperative Council
will "convey the will and ideas of those who are governed
to those who govern". This formula, invented by the secre-
tariat, singularly resembles that used by Alfredo Rocco,
Fascist Minister of Justice, to describe the functions of
the Grand Counoil of Fascism: "Presided over by the head
of the government, composed of the representatives of the
prinoipal organizations of the régime, the Grand Council
interprets the spirit of the masses towards the government
and receives from the government directions for carrying
out

=== Page 416 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
out the work of material and moral advancement."
The committee of 26 provided outline constitutions
for the Movement and the Association. It is only when
the whole project is considered that the role assigned
to those two bodies becomes clear. The secretariat had
prepared a plan of the new structure. It was divided in
two sections. The first showed the lay-out of a complete
corporative state added on to the present structure of
Emperor, Premier, Ministries, High Command and Legislature.
It created two new organs, a Supreme Economic Council and
a Supreme Cultural Council. The former would control
national agricultural associations, "total" vocational and
industrial associations and associations or guilds of
"free enterprise units" which would continue to exist out-
side the "total" corporative bodies. The Supreme Cultural
Council was to have charge of education, technics, sport
and physical training, press and publications, religion,
social works, young men's, women's and adults' associations,
art units. The whole productive and cultural life of the
nation, its religions and its sports, were thus brought
under unified direction by the State.
Alongside this blueprint for a corporative state,
the second section showed the ground plan of a national
body, party or society which was to prepare the way for
the state outlined above, and pass out of existence when
the new structure is completed. This idea seems to have
been derived from the process by which the Italian Fascist
party created institutions which were afterwards absorbed
by the state and became the State.
It

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
It was given a constitution very similar to that
desoribed above. The draftsmen cleariy intended it to
fill the role of the Italian Fascist party which first
built up a corporative state and then merged itself in
the State. Attached to this draft was the plan of a
third body called the Co-operative Council.
The committee of 26 did not discuss the first sec-
tion, which is evidently to remain a Platonic pattern of
the new state until the "movement" has constructed it.
The Supreme Economic Council and the Supreme Cultural
Council, the "total" industrial and vocational associa-
tions are not in the plan. What the draftsmen designed
as the party has been altered into an Executive Council,
and given the task of creating the new structure. It
is defined in Article 2 -- "to set up a system under
which the entire nation in perfect unison assist the
Throne and serve the State in their respective vocations."
The Committee also drew up a preamble setting forth
the purposes of the organization as
(1) the construction of a new order in East Asia
and the world;
(2) manifestation of the total power of the empire
in a national defense state of the highest degree;
(3) creation of conditions in which all the people
shall serve the state in their respeotive vocations.
No trace of "democraoy" has been admitted to the
constitution of the new bodies; they are organized strictly
on the principle that power proceeds from the chief to
his followers. The president is to be appointed by the
Prime Minister. He will seleot the members of the
Exeoutive Council and appoint its higher officials. He
w111

=== Page 418 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
will nominate the members of the Cooperative Council,
but one-half of these must be selected from names sub-
mitted by the Prefectural Cooperative Councils. Those
Councils, and every branch of the Association are to
have the same constitution as the central Council, and
all their officials are to be appointed by their presi-
dents. All Japanese subjects are expected to partici-
pate.
The foundations of a new state structure have thus
been laid. It is Japan's second attempt to shorten the
process of political evolution by taking models and
adapting them to Japanese ideas. To the western mind,
accustomed to regard institutions as the slow growth
of a nation's habits and ideas, the process seems arti-
ficial. But Japan's historical background is wholly
different from that of the Western democracies. The
original Japanese modela are the family council and the
clan elders. Evolution of a modern system from those
primitive models must needs be a long process of t rial
and error and a fascist experiment may be as successful
--- for a time --- as the quasi-democratic system in-
stituted in 1890.
Konoe, however, as his address showed, had certain
immediate objects in view as well as the creation of a
new structure. He particularly mentioned harmonious co-
operation of the high command and the cabinet. Nothing
more has been heard of that. If he intended the new
structure to provide a means of making the army more
amenable to cabinet control, he has been disappointed.
The

=== Page 419 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
The army, speaking through the War Minister, has an-
nounced that it will maintain its independent position
of responsibility to the Emperor alone. It promised
to cooperate with the new structure but refused to be-
come a part of it. Any idea of using the new struc-
ture as a means of persuading the army to yield part of
its power has fallen to the ground, if it existed. By
retaining independence the fighting services have per-
petuated the division of authority which is Japan's
greatest constitutional problem. Whether the new struc-
ture will be more efficacious than the old in reconciling
the powers of the High Command with the responsibilities
of the Cabinet remains to be seen. But though the army
may have disappointed Konoe, it would be an illusion to
expect that the project will not be carried out. It is
needed in order to convert Japan into "a national defense
state of the highest degree." It is the embodiments of
the discovery that Japan, as at present organized, is
not strong enough to complete her plans in China. It
is the product of the belief that military socialism
makes stronger nations than democracy.
Some highly significant facts must be taken into
account in estimating the strength of the forces behind
this seemingly artificial movement. The first response
to Konoye's announcement was the voluntary dissolution
of the political parties. Some of them had eristed for
half a century and had formed governments: others, like
labor, for a few years only; some were ertremely reluctant
to abandon their identity and their hopes. But one by
ono

=== Page 420 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
one they read the writing on the wall, and disbanded
themselves to clear the ground for the new structure.
Still more impressive has been the response of Japanese
industry. Under the auspices of leaders representing
the iron and steel, coal, electric power, shipping and
shipbuilding and other industries, a "plan for a New
Economic System for the People", and a "Plan of an All-
Japan Industrial Federation", have been approved. The
principles of the "New Economic System" are public
service first, abandonment of "liberalistic profit seek-
i ng", and "spontaneous and autonomous formulation of
economic policy". The latter presumably means that
business-men are to be entrusted with the execution of
economic policy; it is a step toward the "Supreme E∞ nomio
Council".
Neither the organized political parties nor the
business leaders of Japan would surrender to an artifi-
cial movement. Their action proves that there is real
force behind the "new struoture". Their example is be-
ing followed by religious bodies, patriotic societies
and all kinds of organizations. Everything is being
unified in order that it may more easily be oontrolled
by the State, and the people are rushing to obey.
The action of the business-men raises a very im-
portant question. If the vital economic framework of
the new struoture is being created by business itself,
if religion and all the multifarious cultural activities
of the nation are being "unified" under government press-
ure, what is left for the "Movement" to do? The answer
seems

=== Page 421 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
seems to be that the actual transformation of the State
is being effected by the Government itself, and the Move-
ment" and the "Association" are the machinery by which
public opinion is to be led. Japan places her own unique
stamp on everything she borrows and in a comparison with
European totalitarianism, it would be hard to say whether
the resemblances or the differences are more important.

Military Intelligence Report No. 10,242,
November 21, 1940, from Tokyo entitled "Comments
on Current Events - No. 38".
The report contains comments in regard to
the following subjects:
Domestic
(1NewStructure: The report states that
althoughthere has been a slackening in tempo
and some criticism in the newspapers regarding
the Ne Structure, It should only be imagined
that"the organizers are beginning to realize
and approach some of the problems involved in
creatin the new organization and making it work.
(2) South Sea Bureau of the Japanese

colonies. Such a system invited the thought
that these lands existed only as colonies of
foreign countries. Now matters relating to
South Sea lands will be handled with unity and
from the standpoint of the creation of a mutual
prosperity sphere.

3. Rioe Shortage.
. second estimete of the rice crop for the presunt year

4. 2600th nniversary of founding of the mpire.
On the loth and llth November were held ceremonies
commemorating the 26o0th nniversary of the founding of
the Empire. The ceremonies were attended by the Emperor
the oourt, and the usual  roat numbers of tuose who received

order and with the usual impressive dignity and simplioity.
Comment: The celebration of the anniversary was mde the
occasion of a spontaneous expression of patriotism which left
no doubt whatever as to the spiritual unity of the Japanese
people, nor as to their patriotic reverence for the Throne
and everything conneoted therewith. The two-day ceremonies
mentioned above, as well as the subsequent days upon whioh the
grounds surrounding the site of the ceremonies, were opened to
sightseers were orowded with expressions of the above mentioned
sentiments and were olearly a spiritual rallying time for a
people beset with many dirriculties. The response was so
spontaneous in character as to remove any doubts as to its
sincerity or any claims that it was built up.

31 October, 1940
THE NEW ORDER - OFFICIAL EXPLANATIONS OF JAPAN'S DOMESTIC
AND FOREIGN POLICIES
The phrase, "New Order' as used by the Japanese was
originally intended to refer to East Asia, and was the term
used in Japan to describe the expected subjugation of China.
During the three years which have passed since this operation
began, the use of the phrase has been extended and since
Japan joined the Berlin/Rome Axis, the "New Order" now refers
to domestic policy, to Japan's plans to control Asia, and to
Jepan's hope to assist Germany and Italy in establishing control
of Europe, Africa and possibly the world. Thus, this term
"New Order" v.hich is used so frequently, is an exceedingly
vague description of Japenese foreign and domestic policies.
No clear explanation, free from politicel verbiage has ever
been given. Instead, the term has been enveloped with vague
generalities; while its score has been systematically extended.
As has been outlined,at first, it was applied only to East
Asia and concerned it self with "co-existence and co-prosperity"
among the strictly oriental trinity - Japan, Manchukuo, and
China. Opinion abroad had no sooner adjusted itself to these
generalities, when ic suddenly developed at the time of the
Nazi invasion f the Netherlands, that this vague formula was
to include a new equally vague area within its scope - the
South Seas region. From th:t, it was soon interpreted as
embracing all of Asia. And now, with the conclusion of the
triple alliance, the New Order has become a world-wide affair,
presumably having beon merred with the New Order sponsored by
Nazi Germany.
Japenese spokesmen htve often attempted to define the New
Order on the basis of opposition to the status quo. This, how-
ever, can by no means define the issues involved, nor can it be
accepted as an explanetion of the New Crder. In cases where
the much-abused status quo works to Japan's benefit, she is
in favor of it. For example, Japan insists on its maintenance
with respect to the Soviet fishery agreements. She has
expressed concern over a possible change in the status quo
of the Dutch East Indies, nd is today criticising American
embargoes as a violation of this principle
A well known retired Japanese Naval officer, Rear Admiral
Gumpei Sekine, describes the new order as "what has come into
existence in opposition to the old order. The ob ject of the
new order is to establish a system of racial existence that
does not exploit others and will not be exploited by others."
He says that as a practical method to attain this object in a
given region, there can be no better way than industrializing
the area and then strengthening the racial consciousness of all
kindred peoples in the vast regions adjoining it, thus making
possible the formation of a self-supplying cooperative body of
nations, which will onter into economic intercourse with other
similar racial groups on the barter system. If such an order
can be brought about, all phenomena centered in self-interest
will disappear. According to Rear Admiral Sekine, Japan has
been proceeding for years - unconsciously though it was -
along the big road leading to the construction of a new order
in East Asia. The industrialization of Japen was the first
stage. The second stage was the establishment of an economic
bloo with Manchuria. If now, China will "cooperate" with
Japan whole-heartedly, the aims centered in the construction
of a New East Asia wiil be accomplished.

According to the official explenation given by the
Cabinet Information Burcau, "The unit within the New Order
consists of a sphere of existence. The movement is not that
of the former territorial aggression, but the lofty spirit
of mutual existence and prosperity. Historically, geograph-
icaily, and economically speaking, Japan naturally becomes the
leader of the Greater East Asia Sphere. This sphere comprises
Japan, Manchuria, China and areas in a southerly direction.
"The present Chinese Incident is not necessarily the
beginning of a new world order. but it has provided a good
foundetion for a beginning. At the outbreak of hostilities
in China, it was thought that the situation could be handled
locally, but the affair has taken on immense proportions. It
is not an engagement fought by soldiers alone, but a major
war requiring the total strength of the country. To cope
with this situation, our established politics, economy, and
whole national structure are useless." It is obvious that
either the Cabinet Information Bureau is unable properly to
define the "New Order" for the Japanese people since they them-
selves do not know what it means, or that they are afraid to
give a complete explanation of what they propose to try to do
because of the possibility that the people will not support the
plans.
As regards the Domestic New Order, a new internal struc-
ture has been deciued upon. It is described by the Cabinet In-
formation Bureau as follows:
"The new structure is in organizution within the country to
unite the people into a single unit for the purpose of forming
a new World Oraur in ob-dienc to the Imperial wishes. To
accomplish this purpose, political parties have already been
abolished, various organizations ar combining and retail
merchants are uniting into logical combinations. This is
hoped to secure a minimum standard of living for everybody,
abolish capitalists and isure Japan's position as the leader
of the New East Asia, as well as the locomotive force in the
establishment of a new totalitarian world in place of the
present liberal ideas. The new structure extends to every walk
of life and reaches into the inntermost parts of one's home-life.
Nothing in variance with the spirit of the times can be toleraied.
Extravagance, comforts and questionable pastimes are to be
abolished. Motion pictures and radio programs are to be more
closely supervised for the purpose of promoting the basic ideas
of this new structure".
Previously: Japanese vere brought up under a more liberal
system, which assumed that one's work anā living was his own
individual concern. Und(r the New Order, this will be changed.
The lives of all Japanose sub jects are now the charge of the
Emperor (cr his advisors) - their wealth likewise. They must
move in the spirit of nationalism in their various occupations.
Then everybody becomes a public worker and there is no distinc-
tion between officials and civilians. Material benefits are to
be minimized, and in their place public service is to be sub-
stituted.
Public health is an indispensable necessity and will receive
much attention. Especielly, will the village recreation centers
be reconstructed with this view in mind. Sports which tend to
build up the national physique will be encouraged.
Report No. 181-40

=== Page 443 === (confidence 0.99)
eport No. 18l-40
/3/
Bright colored clothing is to disappear gradually and in
its place sober garments are to be adopted. Foreign residents
have in some cases already been requested to cease wearing
gaudy clothing. Amusement centers are being strictly super-
vised and early closing hours have been ordered. Expensive
food can no longer be served. No longer can people go out
strolling after dark, for feer thet they are out for amusement
purposes and such conduct is not in keeping with the new order.
Private automobiles may not visit restaurants or theatres for
this is a displey of luxury. Jewelry has ceased to be used for
the same reason. Jewelry dealers have had their shops closed
by the authorities and dealers in art objects are allowed to
sell to foreign residents only. There is a movement to discard
overcoats this winter and already many of the citizens are going
baro-footed.
Although religious freedom has been officially guaranteed
to both the Christian and Buddhist religions, ull religious
bodies will re under strict government control. The form that
this control will take can be realized when it is recalled that
the score or more of Christien denominations active in the
Japanese Empire expect shortly to be ordered to unite into
one group.
Premier Konoe, the Prime Minister, in discussing the New
Order, speaks of lóc.Co0,O00 bodies with one mind. He means
that Japanese subjects will serve the State in their respective
capacities and abandon all individualistic attitudes. Certain
destructive measures are expected to take place, but aiways
the supreme purpose - the establishment of a strong system, able
to withstand the demands of the present ag - will be borne in
mind, accoruing to the Premier.
The following is a translation from an official Cabinet
Information Bureau publication:
"The New Structure was first considered by Prince Konoe,
before he became Premier a few months ago. Since then, his
plans have been considered by scholars throughout the country.
Therefore, it is not the idea of an individual body, but rather
a nationai demand. This demand has been brought about by a
world changing from British, American &nd French liberalism
to that of a totalitarian system.
"The organization is termed the Showa Restoration and is
comparable to the former Meiji Restoration. The Meiji Restora-
tion, it will be recalled, united the country into a single
unit and prevented the territorial designs of England, America,
Russia and France."
"As a result of the first World Wer, Germany, which dis-
played superior fighting ability, received & humiliating defeat
at the Versailles Peace Conference. Japan and Italy: who were
on the winning side, likowise received the same treatment as
Germany at the hands of the threc dominating 1iberal States of
England, America and France. However, Germany was not to be kept
down, and now Japan and Italy in conjunction with that country,
have set about to rebuild this present day chaos.
"Premier Konoe has taken the lead in the new structure,
and his plan is making substantial progress. The first step was
the assembling of the Preparations Committee on the 28th of
August. Members of the Committee were from all districts. The
Committee drew up an organization for the movement on September
6th. The work of the Preperations Committee has now been com-
pleted, and the movement has entered a conorete stage.
Report No. 181-40

=== Page 444 === (confidence 0.99)
Report No. 181-40
/4/
"Under the new system, decision by majority has been
abolished, and instead the Prime Minister and the Government
will make the final decision.
"There are three general principles for the movement,
namely:
 "(1) Based on the spirit of co-operation, it is hoped
to establish a new order in East Asia, and further-
more, to found a New World Order.
"(2) A complete national defense system will be established
by £overnment reform, and through the display of full
national strength.
"(3) To carry out the above plan, all Japanese subjects
work together in their particular sph re and mutually
co-operate.
"The new organization v.ill be divided into functional as
well as the district classifications. The forner is further
sub-divided into the economic and cultural branches. These
two main classifications must not conflict, but rather become
the warp and the woof of one rand structural design."
"The Imperial Assistance Association as arranged by the
Preparations Committee is the propelling force for the new
movement. The Organization consists of:
The PRESIDEINT - becomes the director of the movement,
and is the Fremier. Upon a change of Premier, the
President of the Assembly must likewise change. This
may result in the weakening of the strongth of the
movement, but this is unadvoidable, because the
Premier is in the confidence of the Emperor and is best
qualified in all aspects to head the movement.
COUNCILLORS - elected from the representatives of
the Government and the various districts. These men
assist the President.
GENERAL AFFAIRS - (some are permanent members) These
members examine and investigate important matters of
the general assembly and assist the President.
THE CO-OPERATING ASSE BLY - this is an organ for the
advancement of the organization. Half of the members
are recommended from the districts, and half are
appointed by the Premier.
"The movement will not alter the authority of the Diet.
Diet memhers will become a part of the organization. While the
Diet is in session, organizations of Diet members will be formed
for liason purposes between the new movement and the various
branches of the Government.
"The Army as well as the Navy, will contribute positive and
wholehearted support, and will work spiritually to assist the
new structure. of course, other than the key positions, men in
active service cannot take part directly in the new organization
as this would not be in keeping with the fundamental principles
of the services.
Report No. 181-40

=== Page 445 === (confidence 0.99)
Report No. 181-40
/5/
"Every section of the country is to be organized into
su?-divisions under the main consulting body, and the make-up
of these sections will be similar in all respects to the central
consultation body. The officisls are to be appointed by the
Premier.. ..
"The Diet will be reorganized by adapting present-day
demands to the spirit of the constitution. Parliamentary law
will be revised. The reform of the House of Peers and the
election laws for the House of Commons are now under discussion.
"Various organizations such as the Women's League and the
Boy Scouts will be reorganized as demanded by the times. Especi-
ally will men of learning and experience be invited to take part
in the new strueture.
"There are these who fear this moverent will take on the
aspects of German Nazi-ism or Italian Facism, but that is not
true. Such a structure is not permissihle in Japan where all
persons are bound together under the Emperor. With a single
party, the St&te and the Perty become one and the same thing.
The powerful positicn of the party is everlesting and the head
of the party becomes the permanent power holcer. In Japan,
all persons alike are charged with the duty of aiding the
Imperial Rule and no one person or single party can be permitted
to monopolize this assistence to the Imperial Rule.
"Formerly, there was no relation between government and
culture in Japan. The government had practically no desire to
understand, assist, or guide culture - there was just the
lawful procedure which hed to be carried out. The culture of a
New Japan is to be established. This wiil naturally become the
new culture of East Asia.
"All former liberalism must be eliminated, otherwise it
will result in imperfections of the new system, poisoning the
nation and hindering the program of the State. Japan is playing
the important role of holding the key to the change of worid
history. Pressure εnd hindrances on the part of England and
America are expected, but these must be overcome and a New World
Order established."
* * * * * *
Thus, the purpose of the new state structure is to re-
organize the country so as to try to increase national efficiency.
Increased efficiency is required because Japan is in a perilous
position - internationally and domestically. To this end the
total strength of the country is to be mobilized, re-arranged,
adjusted and intensified. The program calls for sweeping changes
over an extensive field of national endeavor. It will involve
changes in relationship between the civil administration and the
military command, re-organization of the civil service for
higher efficiency, and adjustment of national life on a "rational"
basis. In effect, the new structure movement is designed to
usher in a new era of national efficiency and spiritual unity.
Whether the desired results will be obtained cannot be estimated
at this time, as only two months have elapsed since the ideas of
the leaders of the movement began to take concrete form.

It is unlikely that the Japanese will boycott
American cotton or other raw materials still available to
them as a result of the limited steps already taken by
the United States. The Japanese buy their important raw
materials on a basis of pure exnediency, rejectin
emotional considerations, and resort to a boycott or import
embargo only as a very broad instrument of nrtional policy.
A cotton boycott might be conceivable if further economic
measures should be teken by the United States, end if a
sufficient suoply of cotton for at least & snort period
should be deemed available from other sources. As precedents
there are the boycott of Indien cotton for & few months in
1934, a similar messure ? einst Australian wool in 1935,
and punitive tariff measures against Caneda in 1936.
However, prevailin sentiment in west Japan is e ainst
any present economic reprisals eainst the United States.

Reference the attached undated letter
and enclosure from ir. M. kleiman of New York,
left at Mr. Hamilton's office on December 2,
1940, following a long conversation a few days
earlier between Ir. Kleiman and Mr. Jones of
FE. In the letter ir. Kleiman advocates the
sending of a special American emissary to Japan,
and the 24-page memorandum enclosed with the
letter is Mr. kleiman's analysis of present
conditions in Japan and their background.
(as summariged below)
Comment: r. aleiman's statements are
commended to your attention not because is
proposal is thought advisable of adoption but
because, as a business man recently returned
from Japan, his comments on conditions there
are of some interest.
Mr. Kleiman's approach to the Department
seems to have evolved from conversations in
Tokyo with Japanese who encouraged the idea of
the sending to Japan of a special enissary to
discuss with Japanese a basis for a Japan-
America reconciliation. lleiman states (bottom
of page 5 of his letter) that a few days before
he left Tokyo on October 15, 1940, he had a
long and detailed talk with Ishiwata (former
Minister of Finance) and Count Arima, and
(final sentence of letter) that he is sure
that through Count arima he could arrange for
an official invitation for such an emissary.
The

=== Page 463 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
The memorandum, whioh relates to conditions
in Japan, can be summarized as follows:
The pattern of American policy toward the
Far East has been refusal to recognize any
changed situation on the mainland of asia which
would violate the Open Door, equality of oppor-
tunity, or territorial integrity. This atti-
tude on the part of the American Government has
not been deviated from since l899. A correlary
of the Open Door policy was that sufficient
restrainf was to be placed on aggression to en-
able China to develop unity and strength.
While the policy of the United tates toward
the Far East has been constant, Japan's has fol-
lowed a distinct pattern in deeds and a con-
fused pattern in words. Since 1931, official
and non-official statements of Japan's policy
have contradicted one another; t is has resulted
in confusion even to the Japanese themselves.
The war boom in Japan since 1937 has pushed up
some classes of wages and has pushed up the
prices of commodities even higher, and as a
consequence of these two trends there has been
created a large class of persons who have ser-
iously suffered because of not being employed
in war industries. They have been the mainstay
of the national spiritual mobilization movement.
The banning of luxuries, while paraded under
the guise of patriotism, was aimed distinctly
against the rich and the nouveaux riches by the
"have-nots". This virulent movement against
luxuries has tied up capital in luxury goods
to the sum of perhaps three billion yen. Al-
though spiritual mobilization was encouraged
by

=== Page 464 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
by the Government, the full driving force which
it would develop was not anticipated by the
Government. The mounting unrest wus aggravated
by Germany's conquest of Norway, Belgium and
Holland and gave rise to dissatisfaction with
the Yonai Cabinet, the methods of which were
looked upon as too conciliatory. .onoye aired
his one-party views, but was chided by the
Privy Council and was reminded that the one-
party system would not be a reform for Japan,
in as much as it was practiced by the Tokugawa
Shogunate; the lecture to .onoye by the Frivy
Council spelled the end of the one-party system
in Japan. When konoye came in he abandoned the
idea but launched instead the new structure
policy. The unstated motive in the new struc-
ture movement was to emasculate the army of its
power and to distribute that power among the
people (page l4). To translate into reality
the "people help the Emperor rule" idea, Konoye
organized a permanent committee, known as the
National Movement for Assisting the Throne,
which was in the nature of a junior cabinet and
took into its fold leaders of many groups in-
cluding socialists, communists, nationalista
and any other groups with an idea and a follow-
ing. The most serious fly in honoye's new
structure ointment is Colonel Kingoro Hashimoto.
popularly known as Hashikin. Hashikin was
implicated in the insurrection of February l936
and as punishment was placed on the retired
list; but he proceeded to organize the Young
Men's Association and in 1937 was recalled to
active service in China, where he again made
history

=== Page 465 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-4-
history by ordering the artillery barrage on
the Ladybird. It is estimated that Hashikin's
membership is anywhere from two to three
million including war veterans, unemployed,
nationalists, socialists and communists. it
is Hashikin's boys who are making Japanese
life toe the line; they are particularly bitter
toward capital. The uniform: they wear command
more respect than either the army or navy
uniform. Japan's number one fifth columnist
is Shiratori. :It was Shiratori who sent for
Stahmer from Germany. The consequent tri-
partite pact has resulted in general confusion;
not only did it fail to win popular acclain
but it received only qualified approval of the
army and grudging acquiescence of the navy
(page 20). The most important causes which
induced Japan to align herself with the Axis
are internal disorganization, profiteering,
hoarding and shortages of vital supplies, par-
ticularly food and charcoal; economic paralysis,
stagnation of industry and unemployment; stag-
nation of the war in China and lack of success
in the effort to make peace through Wang Ching-
wei; discouragement over the reopening of the
Burma Road; and the power of Hashimoto and his
group. when the shouting was over the Japanese
realized that the pact with the Axis held out
no hope for a solution of any of the fundamental
problems facing the country, and that it estrang
ed the United States, the only potent force
which could be of any benefit.
Mr. Kleiman's covering letter can be sum-
marized as follows:
Japan

=== Page 466 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-5-
Japan of today does not begin to represent
the Japan of July 1940; within the past few
months defeatism, fatigue and internal demorali-
zation have become so rampant that army and
navy circles fear that the disaffection which
is paralyzing civilian life may even spread
into disloyalty among the armed forces. Capi-
tal, the army and the navy understand very well
the threat implied in Hashimoto's program and
they view with deep concern the moral breakdown
of the populace. In fact, big capital, the
army and the navy, traditional vested interests
of Japan, are convinced that the only hope of
saving themselves from the death knell repre-
sented by Hashimoto would be a rapprochement
with the United States (page 2). Confusion
and disorganization infects not only civilian
life but has spread as well to official life.
The Japanese Foreign Office does not now repre-
sent the factions, both in and out of Govern-
ment, who are desirous of conciliation with
the Únited States and, because they are in the
majority, can truly speak for Japan. Such
reconciliation requires conversations with
at least the aforementioned factions, asserts
Mr. Kleiman, and since in his opinion this
cannot be accomplished through diplomatic chan-
nels some American emissary other than a diplo-
matic representative should be despatched to
Japan for that purpose. The cataclysm within
Japan has brought to the fore the idea of adjust.
ment with the United States even at the sacrifice
of announced policies. The wide divergence of
opinion in Japan prevents conciliatory gestures
through

=== Page 467 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-6-
through official channels but proponents of
this rapprochement are eager for it and repre-
sent every phase of Japanese life including the
Japanese army and navy (page 5). Mr. leiman
says that he had occasion to meet and discuss
this rapprochement with Admiral Yonai, ex-
Premier Okada, ex-rinance Minister 1keda, ex-
Finance Minister Ishiwata, ex-Ambassador Sawada,
as well as with General Araki and Count Arima;
that he was introduced to and discussed this
question with some of the younger army officers,
namely Colonel Saito and Major agayama; that
Commander Yamaguchi of the navy also had a long
discussion with him; that it was r. sotaro
Ishiwata, who was Finance Minister in the nira-
numa Cabinet, who first broached the subject to
Mr. Kleiman when Ishiwata became Cabinet Secre-
tary in the Yonai Cabinet; that Ishiwata is an
old personal friend; that Ishiwata commands
the respect of the army and navy as well as of
financial circles; that iar. kleiman is convinced
that the people who discussed this question with
him did so in good faith; that they represent
sufficient strength to effect a reconciliation
between the United States and Japan; and that
the factions mentioned would certainly agree to
give no publicity to the visit of a special
emissary.

=== Page 501 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISIONOF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
December 23, 1940.
Tokyo's despatch 5154, November 20, 1940,
entitled "The Position of the Japanese Diet in
Relation to the 'New Structure'" refers to the
political maneuvers by the "new structure"
leaders in order to maintain the appearance of
parliamentary government at the forthcoming
Diet session, December 24, 1940, and at the
same time to prevent the session from becoming
a sounding board of possible opposition to the
"new structure". The despatch states:
The session of the Diet was to be expected
in view of Frince honoye's admonition that the
Constitution must be respected, but the disso-
lution of the political parties has produced
a decline in the prestige of the Diet; there
is noticed a lack of enthusiasm for the "new
structure" in the House of Peers which has not
followed the lower house in abolishing its
political parties.
The organization of the Imperial Rule
Assistance Association (which has the function
of transmitting the will of the people to the
Government) includes the provision for a "Diet
Bureau" within the Association, whose function
appears to be to guide and control the deliber-
ations of the Diet; l00 of the 466 Diet members
will serve on the "Bureau" and they will have
advance knowledge of Government policies which
they

=== Page 502 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
ney will interpret. and support during Diet
sessions.
The leaders of the new organization have
been quoted as stating that the functions of
the "Association" in interpreting the wishes
of the people will complement but not interfere
with the work of the Diet.
While it is unlikely that Diet opposition
would be permitted to interfere seriously with
the attainment of "new structure" objectives,
still Konoye is conscious of public respect for
the Japanese constitution, and the forthcoming
Diet session should give a clue as to whether
Japan can secure the substance of totalitarian-
ism without sacrificing the form of parliamen-
tary government.
894.00/979
1.7
FE:Fales:JPS
