Sir:
Referring to my despatch no. 5106 of November 1,
PS/GC
1940, entitled "The New Structure in Japan",I have the
honor to report that the next session of the Japanese
Diet will be convened on December 24, and that its
deliberations will afford an opportunity to gauge
the completeness of national solidarity in support of
the
4

=== Page 504 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
the "new structure". I therefore venture to make the
following observations in regard to the role which the
Japanese Diet may be expected to play under the projected
totalitarian organization of the Japanese State.
In view of Prince Konoye's admonition that the Japan-
ese Constitution must be respected and the "new structure"
organized in accordance therewith, the announcement which
appeared in the Official Gazette of November 9, 1940, calling
for the Diet to convene on December 24, was to be expected.
It is clear that the forthcoming deliberations of that body
are to serve as a testing ground for the "new structure".
If the Diet members are to refrain from auverse criticism
of present national policies, including of course the
whole program of the "new structure", and if the exercise
of the Diet's constitutional functions, particularly in
connection with the national budget and the conduct of
interpellations of the Government, degenerates into un-
questioning endorsement and acclaim of ready-made programs
provided by the Government, it may be safely concluded that
the death knell of parliamentary government in Japan has
been sounded and that the legislature, as a separate branch
of the government, has ceased to exist for all practical
purposes.
The decline in power and prestige of the political
parties in Japan during the last decade, culminating in
their complete dissolution since the emergence of the
"new struoture", necessarily has been reflected in a
decline in the prestige of the Diet where their activities
are

=== Page 505 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
are centered. The system of party government which had
blossomed briefly during the twenties has been in the
discard since the fall of the ill-fated Inukai cabinet
in May l932. Nevertheless, one is hardly warranted in
concluding that the Diet had yet assumed the purely nega-
tive role of lending the appearance of popular sanction
to policies predetermined by an authoritarian government.
Without attempting to review the deliberations of the Diet
during the last several years, mention may be made of the
courageous and outspoken remarks of Mr. Takao Saito during
Diet interpellations on May 7, 1936,* when he roundly be-
rated the Army for its responsibility for the February 26th
incident, and on February 2, 1940,** when he attacked the
China policy of Japan, as indicating that not all Diet
menbers had relapsed into a state of supine acquiescence
in the militaristic trend of Japanese policy.
A lack of enthusiasm for the "new structure" in the
House of Peers may be discerned in the failure of the
political groups of that body to follow the example
afforded by the dissolution of parties of the House of
Representatives. The largest group in the House of Peers,
the Kenkyukai, has released its members from the obligation
to be bound by party decisions (an act which is obviously
not the same as dissolution), but the other political groups
of the Upper House, the Koseikai, the Kayokai, the Koyu
Kurabu, the Dowakai and the Doseikai have not made even
this
* Despatch no. 1830, May 14, 1936.
** Despatch no. 4491, February 19, 1940.

=== Page 506 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
this gesture. The apparent apathy of the Upper Chamber
has been the subject of press comment and it has given
impetus to demands for a reform of that body. There is
not, however, any immediate prospect of the launching of
such a reform. It is interesting to note that party
men of the Lower House, as well, have been accused of
yielding to the party dissolution movement against their
will and only in the hope of thereby enhancing their
prospects for election.
Possibly with a view to forestalling recalcitrance
and obstruction on the part of the Diet, the organization
of the "Imperial Rule Assistance Association"*, which is
charged under the plan of the "new structure" with the function
of transmitting the will of the people to the government,
includes provision for the establishment, within the
"Association" of a Diet Bureau. The rincipal function of
this bureau appears to be to guide and control the delibera-
tions and decisions of the Diet. It is reported that one
hundred members of the House of Representatives will serve
on this bureau and that, in relation to the remaining 366
members, these one hundred members will be in the privileged
position of having advance knowledge of government policies,
in the framing of which they presumably will have participat-
ed, and which they will interpret and support during delibera-
tions of the Diet.
In
* Despatch no. 5106, November 1, 1940.

=== Page 507 === (confidence 1.00)
-5-
In an attempt to refute suggestions that function-
ing of the "Imperial Rule Assistance Association" might
interfere with the deliberative powers of the members of
the House of Representatives, leaders of the new organiza-
tion are quoted as stating that inasmuch as "the wishes of
the people have to do usually with local or regional affairs
and can therefore be dealt with regionally, while the work
of the Diet is concerned with national affairs", the functions
of the "Association in interpreting the wishes of the people
will complement but not interfere with the work of the Diet.
Possibly fearing that this argument may not prove sufficient-
ly convincing, Prince Konoye, in what appears to be a gesture
X
of appeasement, has appointed eight party men to positions
as directors of the "Association".
Doubtless some apprehension is felt concerning the re-
action of the Diet to the "new structure" and the measures
described above seem to constitute rather extraordinary pre-
cautions to insure that the Diet in its forthcoming session
will not prove a sounding board for opponents of the new
program. While it is unlikely that opposition on the part
of the Diet would be permitted to interfere seriously with
the attainment of the objectives envisaged by the "new structure",
still the steadfast assurances of Prince Konoye concerning
respect for the Constitution show a desire to avoid the shock
to

=== Page 508 === (confidence 0.93)
-6-
to public opinion to be expected if existing organs of
constitutional government were hastily swept aside.
In view of this ieference to the Constitution, the
deliberations of the Diet should prove of particular in-
terest as offering a clue to the question of whether Japan
will be able to secure the substance of an undemocratic,
totalitarian state without sacrificing the form of its
parliamentary government.
Respectfully yours,
JI.9rom
800
Joseph C. Grew.

Tokyo's 5163, November 22, 1940, "Com-
ment of certain Japanese individuals on the

made in converoation
The Embassy herein reports comments.with
a member of the Embassy taff by Dr. Takagi
and Dr. Kanda, professors at the Tokyo
Imperial University, and by Mr. Uramatsu, an
official of the Japan Mining Company. Ail
three indicated great apprehension over Japan's
having signed the pact with Germany and Italy.
Mr. Uramatsu gave it as his view that the
attempt by Japan to dominate the South Seas
area is folly and pointed out that Japan could
stand neither the cost of military conquest
nor the cost of economic exploitation once
the territories were acquired; he asserted
that after thirty years in Korea, Japan is
just now beginning to derive profits therefron.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
894.20
Sir:
As an indication of opinion in certain Japanese in-
tellectual circles, I have the honor to summarize comment
recently made to a member of the Embassy by Dr. Yasaka Takagi,
Professor of American Constitutional History at Tokyo Imperial
OLF
University, Dr. Tateo Kanda, Professor of Classios at Tokyo
Imperial University, and an official of the Japan Mining
Company, Mr. Uramatsu.
3E
PS
W1
S/GO
31940

=== Page 517 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
While the western training and background of these
gentlemen as well as their generally sympathetic attitude
toward Great Britain and the United States tend to create
opinions critical of present governmental policies and not
representative of the elements controlling Japan today,
nevertheless these indiviauals' comments on the present
situation are believed to be of interest. Both the China
Incident and the tri-partite pact are bluntly termed
"mistakes", while real anxiety is felt for the future of
Japan's relations with the United States. Japan's economic
situation is recognized as grave and a profitable conquest
of territories in the South Seas is regarded as impossible.
Dr. Takagi expressed his earnest desire for a solution
to the problems of Japanese-American relations. He said that
he was trying very hard to be optimistic in regard to future
developments but that he realized it was up to Japan to show
by concrete acts her desire to reach an understanding with
the United States. Inferring that Japan should effectively
respect American interests in China, he repeated this opinion
on two separate occasions, remarking that official explanations
would be of no avail. He termed the signing of the tri-
partite pact a "great mistake" which had done s erious harm
to relations between Japan and the United States.
Dr. Kanda was extremely pessimistic with regard to
Japan's future. He said that he felt the nation's present
position to be as critical as that of England. He expressed
concern at the increasing German influence in Japan, citing
as a danger signal the rumor that three Gestapo agents very
olose

=== Page 518 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
close to Mr. Himmler were coming to Japan.
Mr. Uramatsu, who participated in the negotiations for
the purchase of the Korean properties of the Uriental Con-
solidated Mining Company, is well informed on conditions in
East Asia. He stated that there were various interpreta-
tions of the now popular phrase "East Asia sphere of mutual
prosperity", including one which envisaed the complete
political and economic domination by Japan of the South
Seas countries. He termed such an attempt at domination
as folly, pointing out that Japan could not stand the ex-
penses of military conquest in addition to the cost of economic
exploitation once the territories had been acquired. "After
thirty years, Japan is just now beginning to derive profits
from Korea," he stated. "It will require another thirty
years to develop Manchuria. It is unthinkable that Japan
could successfully conquer the Netherlands East Indies, French
Indo-China, Malaya, an& the Philippines, and hope to profit
from her conquest." "The China Incident," he remarked, "was
a mistake."
Respectfully yours,
800
Joseph C. Grew.

Tokyo's despatch no. 5?l8, December 14,
1940, subject "Report of Conditions in Japen
During the Month of November 1940".
The despatch reports that Japan is still
seriously concerned with the reaction of the
United States to the signing of the tripertite
alliance in September. Admiral Nomura is
reported as having stated to the United Press
(apparently for foreign consumotion as his
comments did not appear in Japenese newspapers)
that even if the United States became involved
in European hostilities Japen wes not neces-
sarily bound to go to war with the United
States, and he pointed out thet Japan had not
sent military aid to Italy when it became
involved with Greece (pages 16 and 17).
The despatch reports little military
activity and very little bombing in China
during the month, but mentions reporte indi-
cating a concentration of Japanese troops and
transports at Heinan Island (page 20).
Upon his return to Japan from Batavia,
Mr. Kobayashi stated to the press that the
Netherlands authorities had refused to enter-
tain any interest in the program of an East Asia
common

=== Page 544 === (confidence 0.98)
RTMENT OF STATE
SION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
on prosperity sphere but that they would
end an economic mission to Japan as soon as
they found Britain would not be victorious
(pages 23 and 24).
Although all the Tokyo newspapers sensa-
tionally reported the rumored Anglo-American-
Thai pact and there was much editorial comment
denouncing the alliance, only two of the
vernacular papers published the categorical
denials of the pact by the British and American
Governments (page 26).
The despatch reports further statements of
Japanese officials in refutation of recurrent
rumors that the New Structure was merely dis-
guised socialism and communism. The Navy
Minister issued a statement in which the Army
and Navy announced their support of the New
Structure and urged the country to unite in its
support (pages 36 and 37).

The press was laud tory in the selection of Admiral
Nomura. Besides his oualifications for the position gained
from the experience of his past career, including that of
the Fereign Ministership, emphasis was placed upon his
suitability for his new duties, on his having served in the

made at that time with prominent Americans, including Presi-
dent Roosevelt. But the press, however, was not sanguine as
to the possibility of the Ambassador being able to effect any
immediate favorable turn in Japanese-American relations and
stressed the fact that any betterment of the relations was
hardly expected to be accomplished in a short time.*
(c) Withdrawal of American citizens.
It will be recalled that in accordance with the Depart-
ment's advice issued in the first part of October, a number
of American citizens began to withdraw from the Far Tast.
This movement continued during the month of November, and
a number of Americans left the Japanese Empire on the special
ship sent to the Orient to assist in the transportation,
the S. S. MARIPOSA, and on ships regulerly calling at Japanese
ports.**

(f) New Nutional Structure.
Much discussion and negotiation continued during the
month of November regarding the establis mont of the "New
Structure of Japan", but no plans had been completed for
the establishment of furt r int ri pts  f the strueture's
orgenization. Vari oue st tom nts mer made by severnment
authorities regarding the si gmifie mee of the structure and
concerning specific details of its organization. A report
&ppeared that the expense of runnin: the Impe rial Kule
Assistanee Atsociati on would  unt t b ut  100,000,000
per year. This rumor was quickly denied by the h ad of the
Gonural Affairs Bureau of the Asscciation, but he ave no
clear idea as te what suns mirht be required for its mainte-
nance.
The Vice President ef the Cabinet Flanning Beard on
November 2 issued a statement to the effect that the Govern-
ment had no intention of placine all private industries under
Government contro1. Cn Novenber 22, Count Yoriyasu Arima,
Director General of the Imperial Rule Assistance Associa-
tion, made a lengthy statement refuting the recurrent
rumors that the New Structure was nothing; but socialism or
communism in disguise. He stated that these rumors had arisen
from a ross nisunderstanding, and in explanation said that
there was in fact great difficulty in discriminating between
"High degree contmlled economy and socialistic economy", but
that the question really hinced on the spirit and ideology
by which the new economic system wculd be operated. Under
this ideology he cited for example the idea of sacrificing
private interests for the common good, which he said meant the
policy of serving the public first but never implied the idea
of denying private interests.
On November 29, the Navy Minister, Admiral Koshiro
Aikawa,

announced their support of the New Structure and urged that

differenee of pinions as to the erenti on of the New Structure

in early December. The rate of power curtailment is to be
increased from 15 per cent to twenty per cent on an average.

(2) Military Operations.**
There was little military activi ty during the month
under review. On November 13 and 14 Japenese forces, which at
the end of the month of Octobor had evacuated Nanning in
Kwangsi Province, withdrew from the port of Yamchow in the
western end of Kwangtung Province. In the latter part of
November, a fairly large scale offensive was begun by Japa-
nese forces stationed to the northwest of the Hen River in
the Kingmen-Ichang Sector a gainst a large number of Chinese
troops said to have been concentrated there. After announc-
ing that initial successes had been attained in this cam-
paign, no further reports were released concerning the of-
fensive.
Very little bombing operations were carried on during
the month, and there were no reports of further ra ids on the
Burma Road which had been undertaken durin, the month of
October.
Various reports reccived principally from abrad, in-
dicated that there was an appreciable concentration of Japa-
nese troops and transport ships at Hainan Island and gave
rise to some speculation as to th possibility in the near
future of Japanese advance towards the south or an attack
against Yunnan Province through French Indochina.

(f) New National Structure.
Much discussion and negotiation continued during the
month of November regarding the establis.ment of thc "New
Structure of Japan", but no plans had boen comple ted for
the establishment of furtler inteor'l :  rts f the structur 's
organization. Various staten nts were made by v rnm nt
authorities regarding the signifie nee of the structure and
concerning specific dctails of its organization. A report
&ppeared that the expense of runnin: the Iap rial Rule
Assistance Atscciation would mount t b ut  l00,000,000
per year. This rumer was quickly denied by the hoad of the
Gunural Affairs Bureau of the Asscciation, but he gave no
clear idea as to what sums mi,ht be required for its mainte-
nance.
The Vice President cf the Cabinet Tlanning Beard on
November 2 issued a statement to t e effect that the Govern-
ment had no intention of placine all private industries under
Government contro1. On Novenber 22, Count Yoriyasu Arima,
Director General of the Imperial Rule Assistance Associa-
tion, made a lengthy statement refuting the recurrent
rumors that the New Structure was nothing; but socialism or
communism in disguise. He stated that these rumors had arisen
from a gross misunderstanding, and in explanation said that
there was in fact great difficulty in discriminating between
"High degree controlled economy and socialistic economy", but
that the question really hinced on the spirit and ideology
by which the new economic system wculd be operated. Under
this ideology he cited for example the idea of sacrificing
private interests for the common good, which he said meant the
policy of serving the public first but never implied the idea
of denying private interests.

=== Page 670 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 3, 1941
Tokyo's no. 5268, January 8, 1941, sub-
ject "Transmitting Japanese Press Comment:
Editorial Entitled 'The Year 1940' from the
Japan News Week of December 28, 1940".
The despatch encloses an editorial from
the Japan Newa Week, the sole English language
paper in Japan published by foreign interests.
The editorial transmitted states that 1940
has seen the preparatory work on a new political
structure which will eliminete the last ves-
tiges of liberalism in the government; but it
is in foreign affairs that Japan has moved
furthest afield in the new untried road by
Joining the "Nazifascist" ideologies, wnich
action has yet to reveal its practical ad-
vantages, and wnich action has also cost Japan
the friendship of the United States and Britain;
1940 has been a year of confusion and conflict-
ing currents in Japan; no real achievement
can be claimed until peece is made in China;
the most deplorable development of 1940 is
the failure to adjust American-Japanese rela-
tions, and Japan is now recklessly challenging
America over issues not properly her legitimate
concern.
894.00/996

Sir:
I have the honor to transmit a clipping of an editorial
1/
entitled "The Year 1940" which appeared in the December 28,
1940, issue of the Japan News Week. The editorial is in
EEB 1 2 1941
FILED
the nature of a treatise on the state of the nation of Japan
for the year l940. Its theme is what it describes as the
doparture during the year of Japan from the former pre-
copts that guided the nation to embark upon a totalitarian
way
、1

=== Page 672 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
way of life, and in restrained but explicit terms it
critically comments upon this course taken by Japan.
The editorial first presents in turn an analysis of
the effects and implications of the totalitarian movement
in Japan in the political, economic and social fields and
in the field of foreign affairs. In the political field
it states that although the final edifice of the new
national structure for Japan remains to be fully realized,
some indications of its shape can be discerned, such as
the elimination of the last vestiges of liberalism, et
cetera. In the economic field it does not find that there
have as yet emerged such drastic changes as are manifested
in other aspects of the national life, but that in the
social field the results have been the most severe and
far-reaching. It states that there are apparently to be
no limits to which the new control over Japanese society
is to be extended and that the national life is to be
fully regimented and governed by an all-powerful authority;
furthermore there is envisaged as a result of this action
much confusion in the country and fundamental changes in
some of the traditional aspects of Japanese life. In the
realm of foreign affairs Japan, however, has moved far-
thest afield on a new, untried road, in that Japan has
turned its back on, and brought to a stage of serious
tension its relations with, its former friends the United
States and Great Britain for the sake of the sole compen-
sation of "an ideological kinship - and a joint-stock
undertaking - with the Nazifascists which has as yet to
reveal its practical advantages".
The

=== Page 673 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
The editorial then undertakes to submit an e stimate
of what Japan has achieved during the year 1940. It ob-
serves that there were complicated economic problems
carried over from 1939, which continued and were amplified
throughout the year l940. Although some of the emergency
problems have been solved, they have only been solved by
emergency methods which have failed to deal with the fun-
damental causes. The China Incident still goes on and,
despite the fact that the settlement has been overshadowed
by totalitarian reforms at home and more spectacular
events abroad, that issue remains Japan's major concern
from the standpoint of Japan's economic problem. In view
of this fact, the editorial holds, "one is reluctant to
place much emphasis or significance on many of the other
developments". These have been distracting diversions
and have brought about further confusion. Until a real
peace is attained in China, it continues, no foundation
for a "new order" or a "new structure" is secure. In de-
scribing the situation at the end of 1940, the editorial
observes metaphorically that the enthusiastic builders
of Japan are already attempting to put together a super-
structure while the foundation of the edirice is still
unfinished.
Turning to the question of Japan's foreign relations,
it states that in its opinion the most serious and de-
plorable development of l940 has been the failure to
effect a readjustment in Japanese-American relations.
It sees the two countries brought closer to war than at
any other time in their history, and it holds that the
leaders of Japan today are recklessly challenging America
on

=== Page 674 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
on issues which are "far beyond the Pacific and the real
Japanese-American problems", and that those leaders in
this respect are gambling upon an all-or-nothing conflict
between America and Japan.
In my despatch No. 5259 of December 31, 1940, I re-
ferred to the fact that the Japan News Week constitutes
at present the sole remaining English-language paper in
Japan still published by foreign interests and still con-
cerned with presenting from the American point of view
comments on Japan's relations with the world. The period-
ical has a circulation of something over three thousand
copies per issue. Although its circulation is accordingly
limited, it is understood that a large part of its sub-
scribers are Japanese and its couments do receive sone
currency in Japanese circles.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

TRICTLY CUN
With reference to Mr. Hornbeck's
tag memorandum of February 19, 1942,
1t would seem clear from Mr. Hamilton's
memorandum of January 10, 1941, which is
attached, that the memoranda found among
the effects of the Japanese Financial Com-
mittee and described in MID's communication
of January 6, 1942, relate to the year 1941
and not 1940.
In as much as Kleiman speaks of
Count Arima as the one through whom he ex-
pected to make arrangements along the lines
of his proposal to send an unofficial Ameri-
can emissary to Japan, and as Wikawa, one
of the John Doe associates, indicated to us
that he (Wikawa) was a henchman of Count
Arima, it seems probable that both Kleiman's
and the John Doe approach to us were in-
spired by the same group in Japan and that
when Kleiman's approach to us was un-
productive of results the Japanese decided
to carry on with the John Doe approach as
providing a more hopeful string to their bow.

I am informed that there is to take place today in
and
Washington a luncheon meeting at which a Mr. M. Kleiman
the Japanese Financial Attaché in New York City, and
another Japanese are to lay before several Americans,
inoluding one who is a relative or a close friend of
894.00
the President, a proposal for peace between China and
Japan and apparently for bringing about better relations
between the United States and Japan. The general idea
of Mr. Kleiman and his Japanese associates appears to
be to endeavor to cause the Americans to take their
proposition direotly to the President in the hope that
-27
the President would instruct the State Department to
ILED

194
the sending by this Government of some sort of an
unoffioial emissary to Japan.
A little over a month ago Mr. Kleiman called on
officers of the Department and also sent the Department
a letter and a memorandum in exposition of his ideas.
We have not felt that it would be appropriate for this
Government to aot favorably upon Mr. Kleiman's proposal
or that his proposal, if adopted, would be likely to
produce oonstruotive results.

The story presented in this file is
rather fragmentary. Apparently in 1940 there
were various conversations and correspondence
among Nisiyama, the Japanese Financial Com-
missioner, Kamerman, Kieinman, Gracie Hall
Roosevelt, J. M. Davies, Robert Barr Deans,
General R. C. Marshall (retired), Colonel
Mashbir (reserve). It anpears that there
was a project on foot involving a trip to
Japan by Mr. Hall Roosevelt, accompanied by
Dean, to discuss a Far Eastern settlement with
Japanese leaders belonging to different groups;
that Mr. Hall Roosevelt on January lo "intimated
in a casual way thet he might be willing to go
to Japan" and on January 15 among the condi-
tions "aictated to Nisiyama"there was mentioned
a fee of $50,000 plus traveling exnenses. An-
parently Mr. Hall Roosevelt presented to
Mr. Nisiyama a seven-point program for nego-
tiation, but Mr. Nisiyama took occasion to
write Mr. Hall Roosevelt that he knew nothing
about such a program.
The file does not show what happened there-
after. It may be that Nisiyama's veering
away from a program, which is on the whole
reasonable, killed the project. It may be for
this reason that the project fell through.

DEPARTMENT OKSTATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERNA
February 27, 1941
PA/H:
Tokyo's despatch no. 5315, January 22,
1941, "The Organization of the Imperial Rule
Assistance Association".
The despatch encloses the regulations of
the Imperial Rule Assistance Movement, and a
diagram showing the positions of the various
sections, bureaus, councils, directors and ad-
visory bodies, making up the Imperial Rule
Assistance Association, which Association is
the administrative organ by which the movement
will be led and through which it will function.
The President of the Association, who is
the Prime Minister, is through his powers of
appointment in supreme control of the Associa-
tion. He will, however, be advised directly
by an Advisory Body made up of his Cabinet
Ministers, Cabinet Councillors, and the Presi-
dents of both Houses of the Diet.
The Association itself has Advisers and
Directors appointed by the President. Under
this group are the following two organizations:
(1) The Secretariat, composed of various
Bureaus (Diet, Planning, Policy, Systematization
and General Affairs) which in turn are made up
of various Sections such as Economic Structure,
Financial Affairs, East Asia, Propaganda, etc.
(2)

=== Page 683 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
(2) The Central and Prefectural Cooperative
Councils which lead directly to the people.
The broad purpose of the movement working
through the Association is "To Practice the
Duty of Japanese Subjects to Assist the Throne".
It is too early to judge the functions and im-
portance of the various Bureaus of the Secre-
tariat but, in the absence of political parties
the relations between the Diet and the Associa-
tion,as reflected in the functions of the Diet
Bureau will merit attention. The influence of
the Association is expected to extend to the
economic and social life of the nation as well
as its political policies.
There is no statutory basis for the Asso-
ciation, although an ordinance of December 26,
1940, piaced matters "pertaining to" the Asso-
clation and the Movement under the jurisdiction
of the Cabinet.

ment on the organization of the Imperial Rule Assistance
Association (Yokusan Kai), an extra-legal body intended
to unite the subjects of the Empire in service to the
State. It is denied that the Association is a "one-party
movement"; in fact, the political character of the organ
1s

=== Page 685 === (confidence 0.98)
145
-2-
is not clear, some persons maintaining that it should
constitute purely an organization for spiritual mobiliza-
tion. However, in view of the dissolution of the political
parties, the new Association is bound to play a significant
part in Japanese politics. Its relations with the Diet will
be eagerly watched, as these should bring bout clarification
of the real power and success of the "e: Structure" as a
workable political system.
0E1
Premier Konoye in his speech of August 28 referred
to a "nation-wide movement to assist the Throne", which he
defined as not merely a spiritual movement but one aied at
enhancing the political ideals and the political conscious-
ness of the nation. He asserted at that time that although
the movement was tichly political in character, it did not
aim at the establishment of a political party. This state-
ment is noteworthy in the light of recent discussions as to
whether the Imperial Rule Assistance Association should be
an organ of spiritual mobilization or a political structure.
The name of the new organ, now translated as Imperial
Rule Assistance Association, was first announced by Count
Yoriyasu Arima, Chairman of the Preparatory Commission for
the Establishment of a New Structure, at the final meeting
of the commission on September 17. The details of the new
organization were not fully explained; it was stated merely
that advisers, councillors, and directors would be appointed
to assist and advise the President, who was to be the Prime
Minister. Decisions of the commission had been made solely
by

=== Page 686 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
by the Prime Minister, the principle of majority rule
having been discarded, und it became clear that nis auth-
ority in the Associstion oulc be greut. The po er of
appointment of all officials in the organization was
vested in him. Count Arima said in his speech at the
concluding session of the commission: "I want you,
members of the cormission, to agree to leave all matters
which have received your attention to Premier Ronoe as presi-
dent of the projected national movement for construction of
a new structure."
The theme of the addresses delivered at the meeting
on September 17 was the necessity of complete union of
the people to create a "nat onal efense state". Premier
Konoe stated: "In the near future, the situation will
become increasi gly serious. There was a time when I con-
sidered the term 'one hundred million Japanese to be of
one mind' as a figurative expression; but today I am
firmly convinced that nothing short of veritable oneness
in the thinking of one hunared million connatriots is suffi-
cient to meet the situation. So you gentlemen will collabor-
ate, I request, in perfect unison". Baron Iwakusu Ida,
2
member of the House of Peers and of the Preparatory Com-
mission, stated at the same meeting: "Construction of a
political and economic structure is necessary for e stablish-
ment of a high degree national defense state.........The
Japanese spirit should be the guide in the construction of
the new structure. A survey of conditions shows that some
of the people hold ideas which are contrary to the Japanese
spirit

=== Page 687 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
spirit. The entire nation should be inspired by the
sririt of assistin tae i teror in t.e conduct of nation-
al affairs. It is unthinkeble thet there shoula be a
platform for a national movement. Imperial Rescripts
are all that is necessary to guide construction of a
new structure."
The inaugurul meetins of the Iaperial Rule Assist-
ance Association Was helc on October 12 at the official
residence of the Prime Minister. Prince Konoe stated on
this occasion that Japan was facing a turning point, a
situation equal in importance to the meiji Restoration.
He explained that the new Movement aimed at achievement
of complete unity of the nation and discard of liberalism.
He declined to announce a platform for the Association,
explaining: "The basic principle, however, can only be
formulated, I an strongly convinced, as 'The Practice of
the Duty of Japanese Subjects to Assist the Throne'. To
pledge reverently to the Throne the practice of this duty
is in itself the basic principle and the manifesto. If,
therefore, I am called upon on this occasion to enunciate
the basic principles of the manifesto, I must repeat that
it is 'To Practice the Duty of the Subjects to Assist the
Throne'; to serve the Tirone single-heartedly in our res-
pective spheres of vocational activities and in our every-
day lives is all in all. In view of these considerations
I have determined not to publish today the basic principles
or the manifesto."

=== Page 688 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
There was some criticism in the press of the
Prime Minister's vagueness in describing the new
Association and his refusal to present more concrete
elucidation of its principles.
On October 19 the Regulations of the Imperial Rule
1/
Assisting Movement were published (copy enclosed).
The objectives of the lovement are described in the
same mystic terms used in the addresses of the leaders.
The President is to be the Prime Minister and he is to
appoint advisers and directors. Headquarters of the
Association are to be established in Tokyo, with various
bureaus and sections to be set up by special provision.
The President is given p ower to "lead the Associεtion
and supervise the Movement". Funds are to be provided
from dues, Government subsidies, and other sources.
The plan of the Association is shown on the enclosed
2/
chart. The aim is to achieve a close union of the Govern-
ment, Diet, and the people. The President will control
the Association but he will be advised by an Advisory Body
composed of Cabinet Ministers, Cabinet Councillors, and
the Presidents of both Houses of the Diet. The National
Headquarters, set up under the President, controls the
Secretariat and the National Cooperative Councils and is
made up of direotors and executive directors, the latter
being selected to head bureaus within the Secretariat. An
Advisory Council, made up of Cabinet Vice-Ministers, the
Secrotary of the Cabinet, and others, is intended to act
as

=== Page 689 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
as liaison between the Government and the Secretariat.
It is noteworthy that Count Arima holds concurrently
the offices of Chief Executive Director, Chief of the
Secretariat, and Chief of the General Affairs Bureau.
The exact functions of the Central National Coopera-
tive Council, whose chairman is Admiral Nobunasa Suetsugu,
have not yet been clarified. Presumably this Council and
the local councils to be established in each prefecture are
intended to provide links between Government and people,
permitting Japanese subjects in every alk of life and
in every part of the Empire to participate directly,
through membership in local councils, in the Imperial
Rule Assistance Movement.
The bureaus established within the Secretariat are
as follows: General Affairs Bureau, Systematization Bureau,
Policy Bureau, Planning Bureau and Diet Bureau. Several
sections are set up within each bureau. It is as yet too
early to judge the functions and relative importance of
these bureaus. In the absence of political parties,rela-
tions between the Diet and the I.R.A.A. are being watched
with keen interest and the role to be played by the Diet
Bureau will merit particular attention. A glance at the
titles of bureaus and sections will indicate that the in-
fluence of the Association is expected to extend to the
economic and social life of the nation as well as to its
political policies. Many organizations formed earlier under
the banner of Spiritual Mobilization have now dissolved
to reappear as elements of the new Association.
There

=== Page 690 === (confidence 0.97)
-7-
There is no statutory basis for the Imperial Rule
Assistance Association altiouh an ordinance of Decem-
ber 26, 1940, placed matters "pertaining to" the Association
and the Movement under the jurisdiction of the Cabinet.
Its leaders say that it must represent a Movement of the
united subjects of the Emnire, not a systen established
by law. Nevertheless its political character has inevitably
given rise to the issue of constitutionality which has
been heatedly discussed, the proponents of the I.R.A.A. affirm-
ing that the Japanese Constitution is flexible ana may be
interpreted in accordance with changing conaitions.
With the progress of the present Diet session, called
the "Imperial Rule Assistance Diet", the precise character
and influence of the Association may vecome clarified. It
is difficult to see, however, just what its role will be.
The functions of the various parts of the organization have
not been defined. They may deliberate and tender advice to
the Government and to the Diet but the authority of such
advice has yet to be tested. It will be a problem to achieve
harmonious cooperation among Diet, Government and I.R.A.A.
It is proposed to discuss in subsequent despatches
the political character of the Association and its actual
relations with the Diet and the Cabinet.
Respectfully yours,
26. frm
800
Joseph C. Grew.

Regulations of the Imperial Rule Assisting Movement.
Article 1. This Movement shall be one of the entire
nation and shall be called the Imperial Rule Assisting
Movement.
Article 2. The objectives of the ovement shall be:
the establishment and smooth functioning of a national
organization through which all Japanese subjects will
assist the Imperial Rule, 100,000,000 people of the Empire
uniting as one, serving the country according to their res-
pective oocupations; and through this organization the reali-
zation of a structure which will carry out the way of Imperial
subjects.
Article 3. The Imperial Rule Assistance Association
shall be established as the organ which will lead the move-
ment.
Article 4. Officials of the Association shall be ap-
pointed by the President of the Association fron among
persons unuerstanding the spirit of the Movement, and
willing to devote themselves to its effective achievement.
Article 5. The Association shall have the following
officers:
President:
1
Advisors:
Several
Directors:
Several (some managers shall be made
executive directors).
The office of presidency shall be held by the Prime
Minister; advisors and directors shall be appointed by the
President.

=== Page 692 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Article 6. The President shall lead the association
and supervise the Movement.
Article 7. Advisors shall reyly to inquiries made by
the Presicent.
Article 8. Directors shall assist the President and
shall deliberate upon important affairs of the Association.
Executive directors shall permanently conduct the affairs
of the association. The tern of office of airectors and
executive directors shall be one year and they may be re-
appointed.
Article 9. The Central headguarters of the Association
shall be established in Tokyo.
Article 10. To carry out the auministration of the Cen-
tral Headquarters, an administrative bureau or Secretariat
shall be established therein, which shall in turn be divided
into bureaus and sections. A Chief of Administrative Affairs
shall be appointed in the Secretariat.
A director shall be appointed to each bureau and a
chief and several assistant chiefs to each section.
The Chief of Administrative Affairs, directors of
bureaus, and chiefs and assistant chiefs of sections shall
be appointed by the President.
Except for the foregoing provisions, the organization
and jurisdiction of bureaus and sections shall be provided
for in separete regulations.
Article ll. Several councillors shall be appointed
in the Secretariat, and councillors shall participate in
the planning and activities of the Central Headquarters.
Councillors

=== Page 693 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Councillors shall be appointed by the President for
a term of one year; they shall be subject to reappointment.
Article 12. The Central Cooperative Council shall be
attached to the Central Headquarters and shall be presided
over by a Chairman appointed by the President. The term of
his office shall be one year and he shall be subject to re-
appointment.
Members of the Central Cooperative Council shall be
appointed by the President.
Half the members of the Central Cooperative Council
shall be appointed by the President from among persons
nominated by prefectural cooperative councils.
Article 13. Private secretaries shall be appointed
to the President, Chief of Administrative Affairs and the
Chairmen of cooperative councils.
Article 14. Branches of the Association shall be es-
tablished in appropriate areas such as prefectures, metro-
politan areas, counties, cities, towns, villages, etc.,
and a cooperative council shall be attached to each of the
branches.
The official organization of branches shall be determined
by separate provision.
Officers of branches shall be appointed by the President.
Article 15. The term of office of members of central
and local cooperative councils shall be one year and they
shall be subject to reappointment.
Article l6. The expenses of this association shall be
met from dues, subsidies from the Government, and other sources.
The

=== Page 694 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Article 17. The Movement's rules and regulations
as well as amendments to such rules ana regulations shall
be decided by the President.

The preponderance of "Nos" in response to t he quest-
ion regarding the meaning of the New Structure indicates
that in spite of its supposed entry into every phase of
Japanese life, the conception of its concrete meaning
A
is still vague in the minds of the people. A note dis-
7
cussing the answers to this particular question states
that certain businessmen, although answering "Yes" to
the question, openly criticized the New Structure. No
writer or journalist believed that its meaning had been
perfectly grasped by the people.
The responses to the second question were preponder-
antly in favor of a revision of Japan's economic structure.
Opposition to present economic crganization was markedly
strong among merchants and professional men in Osaka.
Sentiment in favor of the economic reforms was also strong
among the small income groups, probably due to dissatis-
faction with their employment under present conditions.
The thirty-nine farmers answered the question unanimously
in the afrirmative.
The

=== Page 702 === (confidence 0.98)
1465
-4-
The replies to the question "Do you think that a
war between Jaian and the United States can be avoided?"
indicate at least thut many Japanese hope that relations
between the two countries will not develop into open
hostilities. Writers and journalists form the only occu-
pational group to exyress a ajority orinion that a
Japanese-American conflict is unavoidable. The majority
of company employees, teachers, engineers, students, mer-
chants, industrial workers, faners and physicians answer-
ed the question "Yes". The division among government of-
ficials was closer, 58 believing a war avoidable, 40 be-
lieving it not. Within the income groups, the closest
division was shown in the replies of persons receiving
monthly incomes of more than Yen 200, thirty-eight of
whom answered this question "Yes", thirty-seven "No".
Among age groups, a majority of those between 4land 45
and over 51 years of age answered the question "No". In
a note following the tabulation of replies to this ques-
tion, the magazine concludes that the large number of
negative responses among journalists, sovernment offi-
cials, and persons in the higher wage brackets indicates
a fuller knowledge of the actual situation but at the same
time a tendency to over-estimate its gravity. A tabula-
tion of replies to this question, according to occupa-

In discussing the answers to question no. 4, "Do you
think the young men of to ay are anbitious?" the editors
conclude that the responsibility for a reconized lacl of
spirit and ambition among the youth of today should be
shared by educators and government authorities. This
deficiency is especially deplored for the reason that a
national renovation must grow out of the strength of youth.
The inconclusive replies to the question regarding
speculation in stocks were regarded as incicating a lack
of experience and actual knowledge of stock transactions
on the part of readers. An unfortunate interest in
speculation among students was discerned in the large
number of affirmative replies submitted by them.
The unpopularity of radio programs was clearly
demonstrated by the number of persons answering "No"
to the question "Do you find radio programs interesting?"
Incidentally, the press reports that the schedule of broad-
cast programs will be completely revised in the interest of
wider listener appeal.
Dissatisfaction

=== Page 704 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
Dissatisfaction in the leadership of the nation is
said to be reflected by the lurge nuaber of affirmative
replies to the question "Do you hope for younger leadership
in the nation?"
The large number of negative responses to the question
"Do you want to get a job on the Continent?" indicating a
reluctance on the part of many youn people to undertake
a career, in C hina, is viewed with concern. Com.enting
on the answers to this question, the magazine states that
the fact that many persons engaged in occupations in which
unemployment has been marked were reluctant to undertake
careers on the Continent, is a problem for serious con-
sideration.
The evenly divided replies to the question regarding
the smooth working of neighborhood leagues suggests a
somewhat negative attitude toward these organizations
recently formed under the New Structure.
Surprise is expressed by the editors at the large
number of negative answers to the question "Has your
income increased recently?" in view of the munitions boom
experienced for some time in Japan. Some readers stated
that although their incomes had increased, the advance in
commodity prices exceeded the increase. The magazine ven-
tured to guess that workers enjoying increased incomes
apparently had little leisure time to read magazines and
were therefore not represented among the persons replying
to the question.
If any

=== Page 705 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
If any general conclusions cen be drawn from this
poll, they are a lack of entlusiasn for the New Structure
and dissatisfaction with present conditions in Japan.
The negative response to the question regarding increase
in income, the desire for renovation of the economic system,
and the lukewarm attitude toward a career on the Continent
suggest that the inspiration of the New Structure and the
Imperial Rule Assistance Movement has not yet succeeded
in dispelling some doubts and discontents among the people
of Japan.
Respectfully yours,

Joseph C. Grew.

steel industry is experienoing many

says Japanese steel industry thirty years

Premier are pro-American.

In addition to the information concerning CHRISTIAN and

MR. BERRY advised that he had been employed in the metallurgi-
cal industry since about 19l4 and that for six years prior to August,
1939, he had been unemployed. He stated that he learned from MR. W.
E. MOORE, owner of the Pittsburgh Leotromelt Company, Pittsburgh,
Ponnsylvania, that MR. F. A. WETHERALL of Wetherall Bros. Steel Company,
Cambridge, Massachusetts, was looking for a metallurgical expert to
go to Japan. MR. BERRY said he later learned that MR. WETHERALL was
handling the American business for GUMPEI KUWADA, President of the
NEHON SPINDLE COMPANY, located in Amagasaka, near Kobe, Japan. MR.
BERRY stated that GUMPEI KUWADA was one of the wealthiest men in Japan
and that the Nehon Spindle Company manufactures spindles for textile
mills, and is the chief Japanese Naval Researsh Plant manufacturing
much naval equipment including gunsights, eto.
MR. BERRY stated that if it was necessary to later contast
MR. WETHERALL, extreme caution should be used to not mentiom MR. BERRY'S
name as MR. WETHERALL is a close friend of GUMPEI KUWADA, having gone
te seheol with him in this eountry and being in elese contaet with
him at this time. MR. BERRY stated that he left the United States in
theearly part of August, 1939, and landed in Japan August 27, 1939.
He said he was employed by the Nehon Spindle Company for eight months
during which time he installed eleetrical furnases for that company
from the Pittsburgh Leetromelt Cempany of Pitteburgh, Pennsylvania
and alse instructed the workers in the manufasture of eylinders fer
airplanes. MR. BERRY stated that during this time he was closely
asseeiated with many high Naval officials of Japan, ome of whem was
Viee Admiral UsUI, whe is the managing direstor of the Mehon Spindle
Gempany. MR. BErrY stated that when he fimished his werk with this

a towm near Negeya, which is the largest loom manufacturing eompaxy

in Japan and also the largest manufacturer of automobiles in the
Orient. He stated that while he was employed here he was closely
associated with TOYOTA, the President of this company and probably
the wealthiest man in Japan. MR. BERRY stated that the automobile
plant is located about twelve miles from a steel plant and covers
almost a square mile. He said the plant employed 13,000 men and
at the present time they are manufacturing between twelve and

MR. BERRY stated that this organisaticu is construoting at the present

in Negoya, which will be completed in August, 19ll. He said ToYOTA
had asked him to return to Japan at this time to manage this plant.
MR. BERRY stated that the steel industry in Japan was experieneing
considerable difficulty at the present time because of the shortage
of serap iron. He said the Toyota Steel Plant was presently manu-
facturing steel whioh consisted of 80% bad scrap iron and 20% iron
oxide. He said that Viee Admiral USUI told him shortly before he
left Japan that the Nehon Spindle Company had only enough sorap to
last four months and urged him to do what he could while in the
United States to have the serap iron embargo lifted. MR. BERRY
stated that Japan was thirty years behind the United States in the
manufaeture of steel.
MR. BERRY further added that from his observations and
conversations with high Japanese Naval officials, he believed that
the Japanese Mavy was very much opposed to their present alliance
with Germany and Italy, and he knew that his acquaintanees fought
the sigming of the agreement for six months before it was astually
sigmed. He stated, however, that the Japanese Army was the eontrolling
power in Japan and diotated all of the policies of that country. Mr.
Berry said he was informed from many sourees that it was only after
mumerous threats and duress that the Emperer and the Premier of Japan

MR. BERRY said he learned from high Haval offieials that

and that he was also informed that Japan had only a sixty day supply
of gasoline and that there is speculation there as to what Japan
will do if they are unable to buy this from either the United States
or Great Britain. MR. BERRY stated that he belioved it was only the
Army of Japan that wasopposed to the United States, and if it were
left to the Navy he was sure that friendly relations would exist
between the two countries.
MR. BERRY stated that he was going to contact the Chieago
Office when he arrived in Chicago. He stated that his time was quite

bs

The Cabinet Planning Board submitted a
proposed economic plan to a meeting of various
ministers who made revisions of the plan, but
criticism arose from all quarters, the Army,
the Imperial Rule Assistance Association, and
business and financial interests which latter
complained of the plan's confiscatory features.
The plan which has not been published appears
to have been drastic and the controversy over
it may determine to what extent the New
Structure will be totalitarian, the Army favor-
ing strong government control in keeping with a
war-time organization.

Baron Hiranuma Appointed Minister without Portfolio.
Kijchur
The appointment of Baron Hirenuma as Minister without
Portfolio caused a small sized flurry in political cireles
and considerable oomment by the press to the effeot that it
was an appointment whioh would go a long way toward strength-
ening the present Cabinet. Baron Hiranuma, it will be re-
membered, was Premier at the time of the Russo-German rapro-
chment whieh took place just before the start of the European
War, and whose government fell as a result of the loeal re-
pureussions caused by the news of that understanding.
As a result of this appointment there are at present two
Ministers without rortfolio, the other being Mr. Neoki Hoshino,
President of the Cabinet . lanning Board. These appointments
have been made under the provisions of a new law whioh allows
three sueh Ministers, and there exists at present considerable
speculation as to who may be appointed to complote the allowed
number.
Comment: Although the Konoe Cabinet has a rather subatantial
record of accomplishment behind it, such as the conelusion of
the treaty with the Rome-Berlin Axis and the conolusion or the
treaty with occupied China and its Wang Ching-wei government,
critieisms were beginning to be heard over its slowness with
anything conerete toward a solution of the China war and some
of its projeets under the New Struoture. Whether suon oriti-
eism was justiried or not is another question, but the faot
remains that, with the so-called ineident dragging on into
its fourth year, all recent governments have been critieized
for their failure to settle it within a short time after
taking office. The appearance of a complicated international
situation, whioh may well have its effeot on any solution of
the Chine war, has added mush to the complexity of the situa-
tion, and with all elements of the nation heartily desiring
fer its end, oriticism on that point appears to be a burden
all future governments are destined to bear until the appear-
anee of some future and fortunate one whion will take ofriee
after this problem has been settled one way or the other.
Whether Baron Hiranuma will be able to contribute any-
thing to the present government in the way of solutioms to
mamy vexing problems is a questionable matter, but, sinee in
Japam he is regarded as a firm character, an able man, and
ome whe has influence with various political and economie
elememts of the nation, it appears likely that his presense
in the Cabinet may make itselr felt in a general rather than
in amy specirie manner.

On the 27th November, Admiral Nomura, distinguished naval
OF

Commmnt: It has become known that Admiral Romura required
considerable persuading by the Foreign Minister, Mr. Matsuoke,
before he gave his consent to his appointment as Ambassador
to Tashington, and it is commonly thought in Tokyo that his
delay in giving his consent sprang from his very natural
desire tat the seope of his powers to speak for the Govern-
ment of Japan be very clearly defined.
The pross has unanimously approved of his appointment,
the general feeling being that Admiral Nomura represents the
highest type of nonest statesmanship, but maintains a degree
or eaution as to what he may be able to do in bringing about
better relations between Japan and the United States. In
shert, the press 1s hopeful but not overly optimistie.
In the above feeling it would appear that the press is
jusciried in its carefully tempered optimiam. With the local
geverament completely in the hands or the more chauvinistie
elememts, and with its mind set on certain objeetives in the
Oriemt eoncerning which it is not yet prepared to make amy
particular concession, it seems hardly likely that Admiral
Bemara will go to the United States armed with any noteworthy
powers to speak with authority along lines whieh would be
aeeeptable to Ameriean opinion. He will thus very likely
find himself in the positiom or trying to explain recemt
Japamese actiom to a pablie and Government whose mind is
already made up, and unless some turn of the intermational
politisal wheel, or a ohange in attitude of the Japanese
Gevermmeat for ome reason or another should take plase,

Pross Interview by Foreisn Minister.
tsuke
On December 9th the Foreign Minister, Mr. Matsuoka,
gave to the assembled press correspondents in Tokye the
first interview granted by him since his entry into the
present Cabinet. As published in the local papers the
interview covered a wide variety of subjects among whicn
were the signiricant statements to the effect that Japan
has mo territorial ambitions toward the Dutoh Jast Indies,
and that "close cooperation with the Axis has become the
guiding prineiple or Japan's foreign polay".
Comment: The interview by Mr. Matsuoka has been given a
great deal of attention by the Japaness press among which
the Asahi Shimbun arrives at the conolusion that its tone
is a quieting one whioh should allay outside suspicions as
to Japanese intentions. This conelusion however, appears
to be one whioh is no doubt on the hopeful side.
A local correspondent who was present at the meeting has
stated that in answer to a question as to the apparent dis-
cordant note between a statement by Mr. Matsuoka to the effeet
that Japan wanted all countries of Asia to be politically free
and the fact that complete politioal freedom appeared difri-
cult under the doctrines or the New Order in East Asia, Mr.
Matsuoka elaborated on this point by explaining to the effeot
that it was expeoted that the policies or various countries
would be made to accord with that dootrine. This of course
puts a very different light on the subjeot.
Whether the statements made in the course of the inter-
vlew have any real significance, or whether they constitute
a statement of the polioy whion this country intends to
rollow, appear equally vague. simply reading the statements
on the various subjeets which came up gives the impression
shat they are merely repititions of what has been said many
simes before coneerning the same matters. The corresponémmt
mentioned in the preceding paragraph has stated that the real
Interest in the interview was the attitude of the Foreign
kinister who talked with apparent frankness, with his usual
tollubility, giving the impression that he was expounding
personal ideas as compared to state polioies, and with an
ir of the zealot to whom the cause and the personal interest
therein are inseparable.

U.B.A.
The nows of the granting of a $1o0, 000, 000 loan to the
Chiang yai-shek Government which appeared almost simultan-
sously with the release of the news concerning the conelusion
of a teaty with the ang Ching-wei Government has produced
a stom of comment in t.e Japanese ; ross and in the state-
mentr of various individuals who were int rviewed on the
subyot.
The general tenor of the comment runs alone the lines
at this action by the United t t s constitutes only an
Aditional proof of the fact of its unalterable opposition
o Japanese aims for the betterment of ast sia, its
support of the Chiang Government with that end in view, and
of its lining up with Great ritain in opposition to the
Axis powers. a good deal of the comment refleots only an
emotional bitterness wherees another section treats the
matter in somewhat more unalytical and objective style.
Comment: The subjeet of Japanese feeling toward the United
States at the present time is a rather complex one and in-
volves many factors of too wide a variety for inclusion in
any brier comment on the subject. In general however, the
opinion is divided into that of a rather small and conserva-
tive element who feel that the present trend of relations is
very muen to be deplored, anc other elementa of a more chau-
vinistic turn of wind who are clamoring for an "up and at
'em" poliey. The first roup appears willing to make certain
concessions for the sake of better relations whereas the other
group is standing firm on all issues.
The above two facets of publie opinion, however, represent
more or less the extremes. The great mass of Jepanese opinion,
while thoroughly imbued with strong convictions as to the
righteousness of its cause, look with no great favor on trouble
with the United tates, and are rather apprehensive as to the
offeots of & possible deterioration of relati ons witn that
nation.
This apprehension, however, snould not te accorded too
great weight in considering the possible courses into which
present Japanese policy might lead the ountry. In the first
place public opinion does not have so great weight in the
shaping of policy as it does in the United tates, and secondly,
whatever may be the coubts and fears of the publio, the fact
that it is thorougniy nationalistic, and the fact that it is
eompletely indootrinated with the idea that in the successful
eomelusion of the China war lies the future greatness of the
mation, will result in its following its leaders in any par-
tieular erisis.
Thus, so long as the present leaderanip 1s able to pre-
vail, it cam be expeeted that it will receive publie backing
in adequate measure, and in the fors of a ready response to
whatever efforts may be called for. The doubts in the publie
mind as to the advisability of trouble sith the United states
are real, but at the present time they are less strong that
the desire to achieve sozething whieh they feel will consribute

2.U.3..
Rumors are current in Tokyo, and on fairly good authority,
that the talks being oarried on in woscow witn a view to remeh-
ing a Russo-Japanese accord are not xoving as smootaly as the
Japanese Covernment would like to have them move. It appears
that the Russians are holding out for the return of oil leases
in Northern Saghalien and a readjustment of the Japanese fish-
ing arrangements which at present the Japanese are not prepared
to coneede. Nothing has been heard as to whether the Russians
are making any demands as to mongolia.
Comment: If the foregoing be true it is apparent that the
Russians propose to make the best of their present position
and will drive a hard bargain with anyone desiring their
cooperation. It may likewise be an indioation that Russo-
German understandings may be of suoh a nature as to cause no
partioular anxiety in the U..s.. as to the implications of
the Tokyo-Berlin-Rome combination, and that they therefor do
not propose to be hustled into any agreement with Japan which
is mot entirely to their liking nor from whioh they will not
recsive full value for any coneessions they may choose to
make.
It has also been reported by the same source that the
Russians are refusing te make concessions in the way of
promising that they will not sid Chine. How the Japanese
reaet to that is problematioal although, being thorough
realists, it is a point which they would probably overlook
in return for a definite non-aggression understanding in
general.
